Joint Sequential Detection and Isolation for Dependent Data Streams
Anamitra Chaudhuriabel=e1, mark]ac34@illinois.edu, Georgios Fellourisabel=e2, mark]fellouri@illinois.edu

Abstract
The problem of joint sequential detection and isolation is considered in the context of multiple, not necessarily independent, data streams. A multiple testing framework is proposed, where each hypothesis corresponds to a different subset of data streams, the sample size is a stopping time of the observations, and the probabilities of four kinds of error are controlled below distinct, user-specified levels. Two of these errors reflect the detection component of the formulation, whereas the other two the isolation component. The optimal expected sample size is characterized to a first-order asymptotic approximation as the error probabilities go to 0. Different asymptotic regimes, expressing different prioritizations of the detection and isolation tasks, are considered. A novel, versatile family of testing procedures is proposed, in which two distinct, in general, statistics are computed for each hypothesis, one addressing the detection task and the other the isolation task. Tests in this family, of various computational complexities, are shown to be asymptotically optimal under different setups. The general theory is applied to the detection and isolation of anomalous, not necessarily independent, data streams, as well as to the detection and isolation of an unknown dependence structure.



and

Department of Statistics,
University of Illinois, Urbana-Champaign,
??; ??

1
Introduction

The field of sequential analysis deals with statistical procedures whose sample size is not fixed in advance, but it is a function of the collected observations. For recent textbooks regarding developments on this area since the groundbreaking work of Abraham Wald
[
45
,
46
]
, we refer to
[
37
,
6
]
. While earlier works, like Wald’s, were motivated by problems in inspection sampling and quality control, in modern industrial and engineering setups it is quite common that there are multiple data streams that are generated by a multitude of data sources (e.g., sensors, channels, etc.), are monitored sequentially, and need to be utilized for real-time decisions. This is the case, for example, in multiple access wireless network
[
31
]
, multisensor surveillance systems
[
18
]
, multichannel signal detection
[
41
]
, environmental monitoring
[
32
]
, blind source separation
[
26
]
, biometric authentication
[
3
]
, sensor networks
[
1
]
, power systems
[
8
]
,
[
21
]
, neural coding
[
33
]
, etc. In many of these applications, the goal is to identify data streams that are “anomalous", in the sense that their marginal distributions deviate substantially from a certain, “normal” behavior. In others, the goal is to identify data streams that share a certain dependence structure. In both cases, the problem admits a multiple testing formulation, in which control of the classical familywise error rates guarantees, with high probability, the precise identification of the anomalous data sources
[
24
,
14
,
15
,
7
,
13
,
34
,
42
]
or of the unknown dependence structure
[
22
,
23
,
20
,
11
]
(see also
[
27
,
9
,
10
]
for fixed-sample-size formulations).

Familywise error metrics are, in general, determined by the most difficult hypothesis problem under consideration. As a result, even the average sample size for their control can be excessive in practice. One approach for addressing this issue is to employ alternative error metrics, such as
generalized familywise error rates
[
5
,
4
,
16
,
35
]
, the generalized misclassification rate
[
28
,
29
,
35
]
, or the false discovery rate
[
19
]
. A different approach is to focus on detecting the presence of anomalous
[
43
,
44
,
40
,
39
,
38
,
41
,
17
]
or dependent
[
2
,
22
,
23
]
data streams, without identifying them with guaranteed precision. This
detection
task leads to a binary testing problem and suffices when an action needs to be taken as long as an anomalous data stream is present, no matter which one it is, or there is some kind of dependence, no matter where exactly it is located.

The first goal of the present work is to introduce a novel multiple testing formulation that unifies the pure detection and the pure isolation problems. Specifically, the proposed formulation requires control of four distinct error metrics, two of which refer to the detection task, whereas the other two refer to the isolation task. Thus, it allows
for strict control of
false alarms
and
missed detections
, without having to either escalate the prescribed control of
false positives
and
false negatives
, as in the traditional familywise error formulation, or ignore it, as in the pure detection problem.

The second goal of this work is the proposal and asymptotic analysis of a general, versatile family of testing procedures that satisfy the prescribed error control in the above formulation.
Specifically, we show that tests in the proposed family, of various degrees of computational complexity, achieve the optimal expected sample size as the target error probabilities go to 0 under various asymptotic regimes that correspond to different prioritizations of the detection and isolation tasks.

The above asymptotic analysis is conducted under a general framework that allows not only for temporal dependence, but also for dependence among the data streams. Thus, the third goal of the paper is to apply the above results to two problems that arise as special cases of the general setup considered in this work: (i) the detection and isolation of anomalous data streams, and (ii) the detection and isolation of an unknown dependence structure.

For the first of these problems, in the special case that the data streams are assumed to be
independent
, we obtain asymptotically optimal rules that are easy to implement, for any number of data sources, under a general setup that allows for temporal dependence. Thus, we improve upon existing rules for the pure detection and isolation problems
[
14
,
15
,
7
,
34
]
. In the general case for the first problem, where the data streams can be dependent, as well as for the second problem, the asymptotically optimal rules we obtain in this work are not always easy to implement. For this reason, we put special emphasis on low-complexity testing procedures, evaluate their asymptotic relative efficiencies, and we further show that they do achieve asymptotic optimality under various setups. Finally, we apply the above results to the special case that the data streams follow a multivariate Gaussian distribution. In this way, we also generalize certain classical results on the problem of sequentially testing for the correlation coefficient of a bivariate Gaussian distribution
[
12
,
25
,
30
,
47
,
48
]
.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows: we formulate the general framework of this paper in Section
2
, we introduce the proposed family of testing procedures in Section
3
, and we establish our general asymptotic optimality theory in Section
4
. Next, we apply the previous results to the detection and isolation of anomalous data streams in Section
5
, and of an unknown dependence structure in Section
6
. We present the results of two simulation studies in Section
7
. Finally, we conclude and discuss potential extensions of this work in Section
8
. All proofs are presented in the Appendix, where we also include auxiliary results of independent interest.

Finally, we collect some notations that are used throughout the paper. We denote the set of natural numbers by
\mathbb{N}
, i.e.,
\mathbb{N}:=\{1,2,\dots\}
, and for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we set
[n]:=\{1,\dots,n\}
. We denote by
\mathbb{R}
the set of real numbers and for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
we set

a\vee b\equiv\max\{a,b\}
and
a\wedge b\equiv\min\{a,b\}
.
|A|
represents the cardinality of a finite set
A
. For a square matrix
M
, we denote by
det(M)
its determinant and by
tr(M)
its trace. For any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
N_{p}(\mu,\Sigma)
the
p
-variate Gaussian distribution with mean vector
\mu
and covariance matrix
\Sigma
, and omit the subscript when
p=1
.
For a probability measure
{\sf P}
, we denote by
{\sf E}_{\sf P}
,
\sf{Var}_{\sf P}
and
{\sf Corr}_{{\sf P}}
the corresponding expectation, variance and correlation, respectively. Finally, for any sequences of positive numbers
(x_{n})
and
(y_{n})
, we write
x_{n}\sim y_{n}
for
\lim_{n}(x_{n}/y_{n})=1
and
x_{n}\lesssim y_{n}
for
\limsup_{n}(x_{n}/y_{n})\leq 1.

2
Problem formulation

2.1
Data

We consider
K
data sources that generate data simultaneously in discrete time and denote by
X^{k}_{n}
the random element whose value is generated by data source
k\in[K]
at time
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For any subset of data sources,
s\subseteq[K]
, we set

\displaystyle X^{s}_{n}

\displaystyle:=\{X^{k}_{n}:k\in s\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{F}_{n}^{s}:=\sigma\left(X^{s}_{m}:m\in[n]\right),\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

i.e.,
X_{n}^{s}
is the set of observations by the data sources in
s
at time
n
and
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{s}
the
\sigma
-field generated by the observations from the data sources in
s
up to time
n
, and

\displaystyle X^{s}

\displaystyle:=\{X^{s}_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{F}^{s}:=\sigma\left(\mathcal{F}^{s}_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}\right),

i.e.,
X^{s}
is the complete sequence of observations by the data sources in
s
and
\mathcal{F}^{s}
the
\sigma
-field it generates.
In what follows, we omit the superscipt in the above notations whenever
s=[K]
, and replace it by
k
whenever
s=\{k\}
for some
k\in[K]
.

2.2
Global distributions

We consider a finite family,
\mathcal{P}
, of plausible distributions of
X
, to the elements of which we refer as
global
distributions. We say that two subsets of data sources
s_{1}\subseteq[K]
and
s_{2}\subseteq[K]
are independent under some
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}
if
X^{s_{1}}
and
X^{s_{2}}
are independent under
{\sf P}
. For any global distribution,
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}
, and any subset of sources,
s\subseteq[K]
, we denote by
{\sf P}^{s}
the restriction of
{\sf P}
on
\mathcal{F}^{s}
, i.e., the marginal distribution of
X^{s}
under
{\sf P}
. For any family of global distributions,
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, and any subset of data sources,
s\subseteq[K]
, we set

\mathcal{Q}^{s}:=\{{\sf P}^{s}:{\sf P}\in\mathcal{Q}\},

e.g.,
\mathcal{P}^{s}
is the family of distributions of
X^{s}
induced by
\mathcal{P}
.
As before, we omit the superscript whenever
s=[K]
, and replace it by
k
whenever
s=\{k\}
for some
k\in[K]
.

2.3
A family of local hypotheses

We consider a finite family of subsets of
[K]
,
\mathcal{K}
, to the elements of which we refer as
units
. For each
e\in\mathcal{K}
, we consider two hypotheses for the marginal distribution of
X^{e}
,

\mathcal{H}^{e}\;\text{(null)}\qquad\text{ versus }\qquad\mathcal{G}^{e}\;\text{(alternative)},

where
\mathcal{H}^{e}
,
\mathcal{G}^{e}
are non-empty, disjoint subfamilies of
\mathcal{P}^{e}
. For any global distribution,
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}
, we
we say that
e\in\mathcal{K}
is a
signal
under
{\sf P}
if
{\sf P}^{e}\in\mathcal{G}^{e}
, and
we denote by
\mathcal{A}({\sf P})
the set of signals under
{\sf P}
, i.e.,

\mathcal{A}({\sf P}):=\{e\in\mathcal{K}:\;{\sf P}^{e}\in\mathcal{G}^{e}\}.

Remark 2.1
.

When the units are the pairs of data sources, i.e.,

\mathcal{K}=\big\{\{i,j\}:1\leq i<j\leq K\big\},

(1)

each testing problem concerns the marginal distribution of a pair of data sources. On the other hand, when the units are the individual data sources, i.e.,

\mathcal{K}=\{\{k\}:k\in[K]\},

(2)

each testing problem concerns the marginal distribution of an individual data source. In the latter case, we replace the superscript in
\mathcal{H}^{e}
and
\mathcal{G}^{e}
by
k
when
e=\{k\}
for some
k\in[K]
.

2.4
A Gaussian example

Before continuing with the proposed problem formulation, we illustrate the already introduced notation with a concrete example. For this, we let

\mathcal{M}_{k}\subset\mathbb{R},\qquad\mathcal{V}_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{kj}\subset(-1,1)

be arbitrary finite sets, with
0\in\mathcal{M}_{k}
and
0\in\mathcal{R}_{kj}
for every
k,j\in[K]\;\text{and}\;k<j
, and consider the family of distributions,
\mathcal{P}
, for which
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}
if and only if
X
is a sequence of iid Gaussian random vectors under
{\sf P}
with

{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[X_{n}^{k}]\in\mathcal{M}_{k},\qquad{\sf Var}_{{\sf P}}[X_{n}^{k}]\in\mathcal{V}_{k},\qquad{\sf Corr}_{{\sf P}}(X_{n}^{k},X_{n}^{j})\in\mathcal{R}_{kj}

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k,j\in[K]
with
k<j
.

2.4.1
Detection and isolation of non-zero Gaussian means

When testing whether the mean of each source is equal to zero or not, then
\mathcal{K}
is given by (
2
) and for every
e=\{k\}
, where
k\in[K]
, the hypotheses take the following form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{e}\equiv\mathcal{H}^{k}

\displaystyle=\left\{X^{k}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}N(0,\sigma^{2}):\sigma^{2}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{e}\equiv\mathcal{G}^{k}

\displaystyle=\left\{X^{k}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}N(\theta,\sigma^{2}):\theta\in\mathcal{M}_{k}\setminus\{0\},\sigma^{2}\in\mathcal{V}_{k}\right\},

independently of the dependence structure of the data sources.

2.4.2
Detection and isolation of a Gaussian dependence structure

When testing whether each pair of sources is independent or not, then
\mathcal{K}
is given by (
1
) and for every
e=\{i,j\}\in\mathcal{K}
the hypotheses take the following form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{e}

\displaystyle=\left\{X^{e}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}N_{2}\left((\theta_{1},\theta_{2}),\Sigma\right):\theta_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{i},\theta_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{j},\Sigma_{11}\in\mathcal{V}_{i},\Sigma_{22}\in\mathcal{V}_{j},\Sigma_{12}=0\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{e}

\displaystyle=\left\{X^{e}\overset{\text{iid}}{\sim}N_{2}\left((\theta_{1},\theta_{2}),\Sigma\right):\theta_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{i},\theta_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{j},\Sigma_{11}\in\mathcal{V}_{i},\Sigma_{22}\in\mathcal{V}_{j},\Sigma_{12}\in\mathcal{R}_{ij}\setminus\{0\}\right\}.

2.5
Prior information about the signal configuration

We assume that the set of signals is known
a priori
to belong to a family of subsets of
\mathcal{K}
,
\Psi
, and denote by
\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
the family of global distributions that are compatible with
\Psi
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}:=\{{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}:\quad{\sf P}^{e}\in\mathcal{H}^{e}\cup\mathcal{G}^{e}\;\;\text{for every}\ e\in\mathcal{K}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{A}({\sf P})\in\Psi\}.

We refer to
\mathcal{H}_{0}\equiv\mathcal{P}_{\emptyset}
as the family of
global null distributions
. Clearly,
\mathcal{H}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\Leftrightarrow\emptyset\in\Psi.

The family
\Psi
is to be specified by the practitioner on the basis of the available information regarding the signal set. For example, if it is assumed that there are at least
l
and at most
u
signals, then
\Psi
takes the following form:

\Psi_{l,u}:=\{A\subseteq\mathcal{K}:l\leq|A|\leq u\}.

When, in particular,
l=0
and
u=K
,

\Psi_{l,u}
coincides with the powerset of
\mathcal{K}
and corresponds to the case of no prior information about the signals whatsoever.

Alternatively, the available prior information may be of topological nature. For example, if the signals are assumed to form a
cluster
, in the sense of being stable with respect to symmetric differences, then
\Psi
takes the following form:

\Psi_{clus}:=\{A\subseteq\mathcal{K}:\;\;\text{if}\;\;e,e^{\prime}\in A\;\;\text{and}\;\;e\triangle e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K},\;\;\text{then}\;\;e\triangle e^{\prime}\in A\}.

Alternatively, if the signals are assumed to be disjoint, then
\Psi
becomes

\Psi_{dis}:=\{A\subseteq\mathcal{K}:\;\;\text{if}\;\;e,e^{\prime}\in A\;\;\text{then}\;\;e\cap e^{\prime}=\emptyset\}.

Remark 2.2
.

When
\mathcal{K}
is given by (
1
) or (
2
), then
\Psi_{dis}\subseteq\Psi_{clus}
. However, this inclusion does not hold in general. Indeed, if
K=2
,
\mathcal{K}=\{\{1\},\{2\},\{1,2\}\}
and

A:=\{\{1\},\{2\}\}
, then
A\in\Psi_{dis}
, but
A\notin\Psi_{clus}
,   since
\{1\}\triangle\{2\}=\{1,2\}\in\mathcal{K}
, but
\{1,2\}\notin A
.

2.6
Tests

We assume that observations are collected sequentially from all sources. At each time instance, we decide whether to stop sampling or not, and in the former case we select one of the two hypotheses for each unit. Thus,
a
testing procedure
is a pair
(T,D)
, where

T
is the, possibly random, time at which sampling is terminated in
all
data sources and
D
is the set of units identified upon stopping as the set of signals.

More formally, we say that
(T,D)
is a
test
if

•

T
is an
\{\mathcal{F}_{n}\}
-stopping time, i.e.,
\{T=n\}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

D
is
\mathcal{F}_{T}
-measurable and
\Psi
-valued, i.e.,
\{T=n,D=A\}\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
A\in\Psi
,

and we denote by
\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}
the family of all tests. We next restrict this family, focusing on tests that control the probabilities of certain errors of interest.

2.7
Errors

We say that a test
(T,D)\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}
commits under some
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}

•

a
false alarm
when there is no signal but at least one unit is identified as such, i.e., when
\mathcal{A}({\sf P})=\emptyset
and the event
D\neq\emptyset
occurs,

•

a
missed detection
when there is at least one signal but no unit is identified as such, i.e., when
\mathcal{A}({\sf P})\neq\emptyset
and the event
D=\emptyset
occurs,

•

a
false positive
when there is at least one true signal and at least one mistakenly identified signal, i.e., when
\mathcal{A}({\sf P})\neq\emptyset
and the event
D\setminus\mathcal{A}({\sf P})\neq\emptyset
occurs,

•

a
false negative
when at least one unit is identified as a signal and there is also at least one missed signal, i.e., when the events
D\neq\emptyset
and
\mathcal{A}({\sf P})\setminus D\neq\emptyset
both occur.

2.8
Testing formulations

False alarms and missed detections are of special importance when it is critical to take a certain action if there is at least one signal,
no matter which
. The relevance and importance of these two errors, relative to false positives/negatives, can give rise to different testing formulations.

2.8.1
Pure isolation

When
\emptyset\notin\Psi
, false alarms and missed detection are not possible. In this case,
we consider tests that control the probabilities of false positives and false negatives, as in classical familywise error control. That is, the class of tests of interest is

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta):=\{(T,D)\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}:\,

\displaystyle\;{\sf P}\left(D\setminus\mathcal{A}({\sf P})\neq\emptyset\right)\leq\gamma

\displaystyle\&\;\;{\sf P}\left(\mathcal{A}({\sf P})\setminus D\neq\emptyset,\,D\neq\emptyset\right)\leq\delta\quad\forall\;{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\},

where
\gamma
and
\delta
are user-specified tolerance levels in
(0,1)
.

Remark 2.3
.

If it is assumed that there are exactly
m
signals, i.e.,
\Psi=\Psi_{m,m}
, where
m\in(0,|\mathcal{K}|)
, then

\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)=\{(T,D)\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}:\;\;{\sf P}\left(D\neq\mathcal{A}({\sf P})\right)\leq\gamma\wedge\delta\quad\forall\;{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\},

since, in this case, whenever a false positive occurs so does a false negative, and vice versa.

2.8.2
Pure detection

When
\emptyset\in\Psi
, or equivalently
\mathcal{H}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
, false alarms and missed detections are possible. If, also, these are the only errors we want to explicitly control, the class of tests of interest becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta):=\{(T,D)\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}:\;\;{\sf P}\left(D\neq\emptyset\right)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\quad\forall\;\;{\sf P}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}

\displaystyle\&\quad{\sf P}\left(D=\emptyset\right)

\displaystyle\leq\beta\quad\forall\;\;{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}\},

where
\alpha
and
\beta
are user-specified tolerance levels in
(0,1)
.

2.8.3
Familywise error control

Even if false alarms and missed detections are possible and of special interest, it may still be desirable to also identify the signals, if any, with some degree of accuracy. This is achieved, for example, by the classical familywise error control, according to which the class of tests of interest is

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)

\displaystyle:=\{(T,D)\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}:\;{\sf P}\left(D\setminus\mathcal{A}({\sf P})\neq\emptyset\right)\leq\gamma\quad\&\quad{\sf P}\left(\mathcal{A}({\sf P})\setminus D\neq\emptyset\right)\leq\delta\;\;\;\forall\;{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\},

where, as before,
\gamma
and
\delta
are user-specified tolerance levels in
(0,1)
. When
\emptyset\notin\Psi
,
\mathcal{E}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)
coincides with
\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)
. When
\emptyset\in\Psi
, it controls for a false alarm/missed detection by essentially treating it as any other false positive/negative. Indeed, the probability of a false alarm is bounded above by
\gamma
, that of a missed detection by
\delta
and, as a result,

\mathcal{E}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\alpha\geq\gamma,\;\beta\geq\delta.

(3)

Thus, in order to lower the false alarm/missed detection rate in this context, the practitioner needs to lower the false positive/negative rate, even if the latter is not of equally major concern, and vice versa.

2.8.4
Joint detection and isolation

Motivated by the above limitation of familywise error control, we propose a more general and, to the best of our knowledge, novel multiple testing formulation, which generalizes the pure detection and pure isolation problems. Specifically, the proposed class of tests is

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta):=\mathcal{D}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta)\cap\mathcal{C}_{\Psi\setminus\emptyset}(\gamma,\delta),

where
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta
are user-specified tolerance levels in
(0,1)
. When
\emptyset\notin\Psi
, this class reduces to
\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)
, similarly to

\mathcal{E}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)
. Unlike the latter, however, it also reduces to
\mathcal{D}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta)
when
\emptyset\in\Psi
and
\gamma=\delta=1
. More interestingly, with a non-trivial choice of
\gamma
and
\delta
it leads to the same false alarm and missed detection rates as in the pure detection problem, while providing an explicit and non-trivial control of the false positive and false negative rates. In general, the proposed formulation allows for an asymmetric treatment of the four kinds of error that were introduced earlier, through the the selection of the four distinct, user-specified tolerance levels,
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta
.

2.9
Goals

A main goal of this work is to propose tests, for each of the above formulations, that achieve the optimal expected sample size
under every possible distribution to a first-order asymptotic approximation as the corresponding error probabilities go to
0
. This is achieved under quite weak distributional assumptions that allow both for temporal dependence, as well as for dependence among the sources. Since asymptotically optimal tests are not always computationally simple when the sources are dependent, another main goal is to propose practical tests, for each of the above formulations, and to evaluate their performance loss, if any, relative to asymptotically optimal schemes.

We next introduce the proposed family of tests (Section
3
) and state our asymptotic results (Section
4
). We subsequently apply this general methodology and theory to two special cases: the detection and isolation of, possibly dependent, anomalous sources (Section
5
), and the detection and isolation of a dependence structure among the sources (Section
6
).

3
A family of tests

In this section we introduce families of tests
that can be designed to belong to the families we introduced earlier. In order to do so, for each unit
e\in\mathcal{K}
we denote by
\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e}
the family of non-null global distributions under which the null is correct in unit
e
, and by
\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}
the family of global distributions under which
e
is a signal, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e}

\displaystyle:=\{{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}:\;e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}:=\{{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}:\;e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})\}.

We assume that for every

e\in\mathcal{K},\quad{\sf P}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}\cup\mathcal{H}_{0},\quad{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e}\cup\mathcal{H}_{0},\quad e\subseteq s\subseteq[K],\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

{\sf P}^{s}
and
{\sf Q}^{s}
, i.e, the marginal distributions of
X^{s}
under
{\sf P}
and
{\sf Q}
respectively, are mutually absolutely continuous when restricted to
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{s}
, and we denote the corresponding likelihood ratio (Radon-Nikodym derivative) and log-likelihood ratio as follows:

\Lambda_{n}({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}):=\frac{d{\sf P}^{s}}{d{\sf Q}^{s}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{n}^{s}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad Z_{n}({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}):=\log\Lambda_{n}({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}).

3.1
Detection and isolation statistics

For each unit
e\in\mathcal{K}
we select two subsets of
[K]
,
s_{e},s^{\prime}_{e}\subseteq[K]
, that both include
e
, i.e.,
e\subseteq s_{e}\cap s^{\prime}_{e},

to which we refer as the
subsystems of unit
e
. Then, at each time
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we compute the values of the following GLR-type statistics,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{e,\rm det}(n)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\max\{\Lambda_{n}({\sf P},{\sf P}_{0}):{\sf P}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}^{s_{e}}\}}{\max\{\Lambda_{n}({\sf P},{\sf P}_{0}):{\sf P}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{s_{e}}\}},\quad\quad\Lambda_{e,\rm iso}(n):=\frac{\max\{\Lambda_{n}({\sf P},{\sf P}^{\prime}_{0}):{\sf P}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}^{s^{\prime}_{e}}\}}{\max\{\Lambda_{n}({\sf P},{\sf P}^{\prime}_{0}):{\sf P}\in\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e}^{s^{\prime}_{e}}\}},

where
{\sf P}_{0}
and
{\sf P}^{\prime}_{0}
are arbitrary distributions from
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{s_{e}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{0}^{s^{\prime}_{e}}
, respectively. We note that

•

\Lambda_{e,\rm det}(n)
is based on data from
s_{e}
up to time
n
and the larger its value, the stronger the evidence that there exists
at least one signal
. Thus, we refer to
s_{e}
as the
detection subsystem
for unit
e
and to
\Lambda_{e,\rm det}
as the
detection
statistic for unit
e
.

•

\Lambda_{e,\rm iso}(n)
is based on data from
s^{\prime}_{e}
up to time
n
, and the larger its value, the stronger the evidence that unit
e

is a signal
. Thus, we refer to
s^{\prime}_{e}
as the
isolation subsystem
for unit
e
and to
\Lambda_{e,\rm iso}
as the
isolation
statistic for unit
e
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
we also denote by
D_{\rm iso}(n)
the set of units whose isolation statistics at time
n
have values larger than
1
, i.e.,

D_{\rm iso}(n):=\left\{e\in\mathcal{K}:\Lambda_{e,\rm iso}(n)>1\right\}.

(4)

Therefore,
D_{\rm iso}(n)
can be interpreted as the estimated signal set based on the from the isolation subsystems in the first
n
time instances.

When the detection and isolation subsystems of unit
e\in\mathcal{K}
are selected as small as possible, i.e.,
s_{e}=s^{\prime}_{e}=e
, then its isolation and detection statistics coincide and reduce to

\displaystyle\Lambda_{e}(n):=\frac{\max\{\Lambda_{n}({\sf P},{\sf P}_{0}):{\sf P}\in\mathcal{G}^{e}\}}{\max\{\Lambda_{n}({\sf P},{\sf P}_{0}):{\sf P}\in\mathcal{H}^{e}\}}.

(5)

We refer to
\Lambda_{e}(n)
as the
local
statistic of unit
e
at time
n
, as it is computed based solely on data from unit
e
up to time
n
.
Furthermore, we denote the ordered versions of the local statistics by
\Lambda_{(1)}(n)\geq\dots\geq\Lambda_{(|\mathcal{K}|)}(n)
, and the corresponding indices by
i_{1}(n),\dots,i_{|\mathcal{K}|}(n)
. We also denote by
p(n)
the number of units whose local statistics at time
n
have values larger than
1
, i.e.,

p(n):=|\{e\in\mathcal{K}:\Lambda_{e}(n)>1\}|
.

Remark 3.1
.

When
e=\{k\}
for some
k\in[K]
,
we replace
e
in
\Lambda_{e,\rm det}(n)
,
\Lambda_{e,\rm iso}(n)
,
\Lambda_{e}(n)
by
k
, and write
\Lambda_{k,\rm det}(n)
,
\Lambda_{k,\rm iso}(n)
,
\Lambda_{k}(n)
, instead.

3.2
The proposed tests

We next utilize the above statistics to devise tests that belong to each of the families we introduced in the previous section.

3.2.1
The test for pure isolation

When
\emptyset\notin\Psi
, we stop sampling when the value of every
isolation
statistic is either too large or too small, and we identify as signals those units for which these values are large. Therefore, the proposed test in this case,
\chi^{*}:=(T^{*},D^{*})
, is:

\displaystyle T^{*}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:D_{\rm iso}(n)\in\Psi,\;\Lambda_{e,\rm iso}(n)\notin\left(1/A_{e},B_{e}\right)\;\forall\;e\in\mathcal{K}\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad D^{*}:=D_{\rm iso}(T^{*}),

where
\{A_{e},B_{e}\}_{e\in\mathcal{K}}
are thresholds to be determined so that
\chi^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)
.

3.2.2
The test for pure detection

When
\emptyset\in\Psi
and the focus is on the pure detection problem, we stop sampling when either all detection statistics are small, in which case the null hypothesis is selected in all units, or
at least one
detection statistic is large enough, in which case some non-empty subset of units is identified as the set of signals.
Specifically, the proposed test in this case,

\chi^{*}_{det}:=(T^{*}_{det},D^{*}_{det})
, is:

\displaystyle T^{*}_{det}

\displaystyle:=\min\{T_{0},T_{det}\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad D_{det}^{*}=\emptyset\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad T_{0}<T_{det},

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad T_{0}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\Lambda_{e,\rm det}(n)\leq 1/C_{e}\quad\text{for every}\ e\in\mathcal{K}\right\},

\displaystyle T_{det}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\Lambda_{e,\rm det}(n)\geq D_{e}\quad\text{for some}\ e\in\mathcal{K}\right\},

and
\{C_{e},D_{e}\}_{e\in\mathcal{K}}
are thresholds to be determined so that
\chi^{*}_{det}\in\mathcal{D}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta)
.

3.2.3
The test for joint detection and isolation

When
\emptyset\in\Psi
and the goal is to control all four kinds of error, the proposed test in this case,
\chi^{*}:=\left(T^{*},D^{*}\right)
, is based on a combination of the two previous ones:

\displaystyle T^{*}

\displaystyle:=\min\{T_{0},T_{joint}\},\qquad D^{*}:=\begin{cases}\emptyset\ &\text{if}\quad T_{0}<T_{joint}\\
D_{\rm iso}\left(T^{*}\right)\ &\text{if}\quad T_{joint}<T_{0}\end{cases},

where
T_{0}
is defined as before,

\displaystyle T_{joint}:=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}:

\displaystyle\;\Lambda_{e,\rm det}(n)\geq D_{e}\quad\text{for some}\ e\in\mathcal{K},

\displaystyle\Lambda_{e,\rm iso}(n)\notin\left(1/A_{e},B_{e}\right)\quad\text{for every}\,e\in\mathcal{K},\quad D_{\rm iso}(n)\in\Psi\setminus\emptyset\},

and
\{A_{e},B_{e},C_{e},D_{e}\}_{e\in\mathcal{K}}
are thresholds to be determined so that
\chi^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)
.

3.2.4
The test for familywise error control

When
\emptyset\in\Psi
and we are interested in the familywise error control of Subsection
2.8.3
,
we utilize

\chi^{*}:=\left(T^{*},D^{*}\right)\quad\text{with}\quad A_{e}=C_{e},B_{e}=D_{e}\quad\text{for every}\;e\in\mathcal{K},

and denote the resulting test by

\chi_{fwer}^{*}:=(T_{fwer}^{*},D_{fwer}^{*}).

Remark 3.2
.

When

s_{e}=s^{\prime}_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}T^{*}_{fwer}&=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\,D_{\rm iso}(n)\in\Psi,\;\Lambda_{e}(n)\notin\left(1/A_{e},B_{e}\right)\;\forall\;e\in\mathcal{K}\},\\
D^{*}_{fwer}&=D_{\rm iso}(T^{*}_{fwer})\end{split}

(6)

and if also
\Psi
is the power set of
\mathcal{K}
,

\chi^{*}_{fwer}
coincides with the
“Intersection Rule”
in
[
15
,
14
]
.

3.3
Error control

We next select the thresholds in each of the above tests in order to satisfy the desired error constraints. We emphasize that no additional distributional assumptions are needed for this purpose. To state these results, we set

a_{e}:=|\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e}^{s^{\prime}_{e}}|,\quad b_{e}:=|\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}^{s^{\prime}_{e}}|,\quad c_{e}:=|\mathcal{H}_{0}^{s_{e}}|,\quad d_{e}:=|\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}^{s_{e}}|\quad e\in\mathcal{K}.

(7)

Theorem 3.1
.

Suppose that
\emptyset\notin\Psi
and let

\gamma,\delta\in(0,1)
.

(i)

\chi^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)
when

\displaystyle A_{e}\geq a_{e}\,|\mathcal{K}|/\delta\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{e}\geq b_{e}\,|\mathcal{K}|/\gamma\quad\forall\,e\in\mathcal{K}.

(ii)

If, also,
\Psi=\Psi_{m,m}
for some
m\in(0,|\mathcal{K}|)
, then
\chi^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)
when

\displaystyle A_{e}\geq a_{e}\,|\mathcal{K}|/(\gamma\wedge\delta)\quad\text{and}\qquad B_{e}\geq b_{e}\,|\mathcal{K}|/(\gamma\wedge\delta)\quad\forall\,e\in\mathcal{K}.

The proof can be found in Appendix
A.2
.
∎

Theorem 3.2
.

Suppose that
\emptyset\in\Psi
and let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in(0,1)
.

(i)

\chi^{*}_{det}\in\mathcal{D}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta)
when

\displaystyle C_{e}\geq c_{e}/\beta\qquad\text{and}\qquad D_{e}\geq d_{e}\,|\mathcal{K}|/\alpha\quad\forall\,e\in\mathcal{K}.

(ii)

\chi_{fwer}^{*}\in\mathcal{E}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)
when

\displaystyle A_{e}

\displaystyle\geq(a_{e}\vee c_{e})\,(|\mathcal{K}|+1)/\delta\qquad\text{and }\qquad B_{e}\geq(b_{e}\vee d_{e})\,|\mathcal{K}|/\gamma\quad\forall\,e\in\mathcal{K}.

If, also,
s_{e}=s^{\prime}_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
, then

\chi_{fwer}^{*}\in\mathcal{E}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)
when

\displaystyle A_{e}\geq a_{e}\,|\mathcal{K}|/\delta\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{e}\geq b_{e}\,|\mathcal{K}|/\gamma\quad\forall\,e\in\mathcal{K}.

(iii)

\chi^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)
when
\{C_{e},D_{e}\}_{e\in\mathcal{K}}
are as in (i) and
\{A_{e},B_{e}\}_{e\in\mathcal{K}}
as in Theorem
3.1
.

The proof can be found in Appendix
A.3
.
∎

3.4
Selection of thresholds

Based on Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
, in what follows we assume that

•

when
\emptyset\notin\Psi
,
\{A_{e},B_{e}\}_{e\in\mathcal{K}}
are selected, as functions of
\gamma,\delta\in(0,1)
, so that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\chi^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)\quad\forall\;\gamma,\delta\in(0,1),\\
&\log A_{e}\sim|\log\delta|,\quad\log B_{e}\sim|\log\gamma|\quad\forall\;e\in\mathcal{K}\quad\text{as}\;\;\gamma,\delta\to 0,\end{split}

(8)

•

when
\emptyset\in\Psi
,
\{A_{e},B_{e},C_{e},D_{e}\}_{e\in\mathcal{K}}
are selected, as functions of

\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in(0,1)
, so that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\chi^{*}_{det}\in\mathcal{D}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta),\quad\chi^{*}_{fwer}\in\mathcal{E}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta),\quad\chi^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)\quad\forall\,\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in(0,1),\\
&\log A_{e}\sim|\log\delta|,\quad\log B_{e}\sim|\log\gamma|,\quad\log C_{e}\sim|\log\beta|,\quad\log D_{e}\sim|\log\alpha|\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\forall\;\ e\in\mathcal{K}\quad\text{as}\quad\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0.\end{split}

(9)

We further assume that

A_{e}=A,\quad B_{e}=B,\quad C_{e}=C,\quad D_{e}=D\quad\text{ for every }e\in\mathcal{K}.

(10)

The latter is not needed for any of the results in Section
4
, but simplifies the implementation of the proposed tests in Section
5.1
, and is also used in Theorem
6.1
.

4
Asymptotic optimality

In this section we show that, at least when the detection and/or isolation subsystems are large enough, the tests introduced in the previous section achieve the minimum expected number of observations until stopping, simultaneously for every
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
, to a first-order asymptotic approximation as the corresponding error probabilities go to 0. Moreover, we establish sufficient conditions for asymptotic optimality using smaller subsystems. All these results are established under weak distributional assumptions, which allow both for temporal dependence as well as for dependence among the sources.

4.1
Distributional assumptions

To state the main results of this section we assume that, for every

e\in\mathcal{K},\quad{\sf P}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e},\quad{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e}\cup\mathcal{H}_{0},\quad e\subseteq s\subseteq[K],

there are positive numbers,
\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}\right)
and
\mathcal{I}\left({\sf Q}^{s},{\sf P}^{s}\right)
, such that as
n\to\infty

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\sf P}\left(\max_{1\leq m\leq n}Z_{m}\left({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}\right)\geq n\rho\right)\to 0\quad\text{for all}\;\;\rho>\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}\right),\\
&{\sf Q}\left(\max_{1\leq m\leq n}Z_{m}\left({\sf Q}^{s},{\sf P}^{s}\right)\geq n\rho\right)\to 0\quad\text{for all}\;\;\rho>\mathcal{I}\left({\sf Q}^{s},{\sf P}^{s}\right),\end{split}

(11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\sf P}\left(Z_{n}\left({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}\right)<n\rho\right)<\infty\quad\text{for all}\;\;\rho<\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}\right),\\
&\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\sf Q}\left(Z_{n}\left({\sf Q}^{s},{\sf P}^{s}\right)<n\rho\right)<\infty\quad\text{for all}\;\;\rho<\mathcal{I}\left({\sf Q}^{s},{\sf P}^{s}\right).\end{split}

(12)

Conditions (
11
) and (
12
) imply that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}Z_{n}\left({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{{\sf P}}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{1}{n}Z_{n}\left({\sf Q}^{s},{\sf P}^{s}\right)\overset{{\sf Q}}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf Q}^{s},{\sf P}^{s}\right)

(13)

thus, the limits,
\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}\right)
and
\mathcal{I}\left({\sf Q}^{s},{\sf P}^{s}\right)
, can be interpreted as (Kullback-Leibler) divergences between the global distributions
{\sf P}
and
{\sf Q}
based on the data from the sources in
s
. Several useful properties regarding these numbers are presented in Appendix
B
.

Remark 4.1
.

When the convergences in (
13
) hold not only in probability but also almost surely,
then condition (
11
) is satisfied, but not necessarily (
12
).

Remark 4.2
.

If
X^{s}
is an iid sequence and

Z_{1}\left({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}\right)
has
non-zero, finite
expectation under both
{\sf P}
and
{\sf Q}
, then conditions (
11
)-(
12
) hold with

\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}\right)={\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[Z_{1}\left({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}\right)]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{I}\left({\sf Q}^{s},{\sf P}^{s}\right)={\sf E}_{{\sf Q}}[Z_{1}\left({\sf Q}^{s},{\sf P}^{s}\right)].

Indeed, (
13
) holds with almost sure convergence by Kolmogorov’s Strong Law of Large Numbers, and (
12
) by the Chernoff bound. As a result, in the special case of Subsection
2.4
, conditions (
11
)-(
12
) hold with

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s})&=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{det(\Sigma_{\sf Q})}{det(\Sigma_{\sf P})}-|s|+tr(\Sigma_{\sf Q}^{-1}\Sigma_{\sf P})+(\mu_{\sf Q}-\mu_{\sf P})^{t}\Sigma_{\sf Q}^{-1}(\mu_{\sf Q}-\mu_{\sf P})\right\},\\
\mathcal{I}({\sf Q}^{s},{\sf P}^{s})&=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{det(\Sigma_{\sf P})}{det(\Sigma_{\sf Q})}-|s|+tr(\Sigma_{\sf P}^{-1}\Sigma_{\sf Q})+(\mu_{\sf P}-\mu_{\sf Q})^{t}\Sigma_{\sf P}^{-1}(\mu_{\sf P}-\mu_{\sf Q})\right\},\end{split}

(14)

where
\mu_{\sf P}
(resp.
\mu_{\sf Q}
) is the mean vector and
\Sigma_{\sf P}
(resp.
\Sigma_{\sf Q}
) the covariance matrix of
X_{1}^{s}
under
{\sf P}
(resp.
{\sf Q}
). In general, when
X^{s}
is non-iid, these conditions hold under various models, such as autoregressive time-series models, state-space models, etc., see
[
37
, Section 3.4]
for more details.

4.2
Notations

For any
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
,
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
and
s\subseteq[K]
we set

\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{s},\mathcal{Q}^{s}):=\inf_{{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s}),

whenever the quantities in the right-hand side are well defined. We also set

\mathcal{I}({\sf P},{\sf Q};s):=\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{s},{\sf Q}^{s})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{Q};s):=\min_{{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{I}({\sf P},{\sf Q};s).

As before, we omit
s
when
s=[K]
and replace it by
k
whenever
s=\{k\}
for some
k\in[K]
.

Example 4.1
.

Consider the setup of Subsection
2.4.1
, fix
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
and, for each
k\in[K]
denote by
\sigma_{k}^{2}
the variance of
X_{1}^{k}
under

{\sf P}
. Moreover, denote by
\mu_{k}
the expectation of
X_{1}^{k}
under

{\sf P}
when
\{k\}\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{k},\mathcal{H}^{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\mu_{k}}{\sigma_{k}}\right)^{2}\qquad\text{if}\;\;\{k\}\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{k},\mathcal{G}^{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\;\min_{\theta\in\mathcal{M}_{k}\setminus\{0\}}\left(\frac{\theta}{\sigma_{k}}\right)^{2}\qquad\text{if}\;\;\{k\}\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P}).

Example 4.2
.

Consider the setup of Subsection
2.4.2

with
\mathcal{R}_{kj}
equal to either
\{0,\rho\}
or
\{0,\pm\rho\}
for every
k,j\in[K]
with
k<j
, for some
\rho\in(0,1)
. Then, for any
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{H}^{e})

\displaystyle=-\log\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}\qquad\forall\;e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{G}^{e})

\displaystyle=\log\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{1-\rho^{2}}\qquad\forall\;e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P}).

4.3
Upper bound on the expected sample size

We continue with an asymptotic upper bound on the expected sample size of the proposed tests as their thresholds go to infinity.

Theorem 4.1
.

Suppose that (
12
) holds,
\emptyset\notin\Psi
, and let
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
. Then, as
\gamma,\delta\to 0
,

{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}\right]\lesssim\max_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\gamma|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e};s^{\prime}_{e}\right)}\right\}\bigvee\max_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\delta|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e};s^{\prime}_{e}\right)}\right\}.

The proof can be found in Appendix
C.1
.
∎

Theorem 4.2
.

Suppose that (
12
) holds,
\emptyset\in\Psi
, and let
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
.

(i)

If
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
, then

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}_{det}\right],{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\;\max_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\left\{\frac{|\log\beta|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e};s_{e}\right)}\right\}\quad\text{as}\quad\beta\to 0,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}_{fwer}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\;\max_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\left\{\frac{|\log\delta|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e};s_{e}\right)}\right\}\quad\text{as}\quad\delta\to 0.

(ii)

If
{\sf P}\notin\mathcal{H}_{0}
, then

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}_{det}\right]\lesssim

\displaystyle\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\alpha|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0};s_{e}\right)}\right\}\quad\text{as}\quad\alpha\to 0,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}_{fwer}\right]\lesssim

\displaystyle\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\gamma|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0};s_{e}\right)}\right\}\bigvee\max_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\gamma|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e};s^{\prime}_{e}\right)}\right\}

\displaystyle\bigvee\max_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\delta|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e};s^{\prime}_{e}\right)}\right\}\quad\text{as}\quad\gamma,\delta\to 0,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}\right]\lesssim

\displaystyle\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\alpha|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0};s_{e}\right)}\right\}\bigvee\max_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\gamma|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e};s^{\prime}_{e}\right)}\right\}

\displaystyle\bigvee\max_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\delta|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e};s^{\prime}_{e}\right)}\right\}\quad\text{as}\quad\alpha,\gamma,\delta\to 0.

The proof can be found in Appendix
C.1
.
∎

4.4
Asymptotic optimality

We next show that the proposed tests achieve the optimal expected sample size in their corresponding classes, to a first-order asymptotic approximation as the corresponding error rates go to
0
, when the detection and/or isolation subsystems are selected to be equal to
[K]
, and in certain cases even smaller than
[K]
. Our standing assumption in the remainder of this section is that conditions (
11
)-(
12
) hold.

We start with the case that
\emptyset\notin\Psi
, where there is no detection task and the only test under consideration is
\chi^{*}
, defined in Subsection
3.2.1
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Suppose that
\emptyset\notin\Psi
,
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
, and let the isolation subsystems be selected such that

\displaystyle\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e};s^{\prime}_{e}\right)

\displaystyle=\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e}\right),

(15)

\displaystyle\min_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e};s^{\prime}_{e}\right)

\displaystyle=\min_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}\right),

(16)

which is trivially the case when
s^{\prime}_{e}=[K]
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
.
Then, as
\gamma,\delta\to 0
,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}\right]

\displaystyle\sim\max_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\gamma|}{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e})}\right\}\bigvee\max_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\delta|}{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e})}\right\}

\displaystyle\sim\inf\left\{{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[T]:\;(T,D)\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)\right\}.

When, in particular,
\Psi=\Psi_{m,m}
for some
m\in(0,|\mathcal{K}|)
,
then, as
\gamma,\delta\to 0
,

{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}\right]\sim\frac{|\log(\gamma\wedge\delta)|}{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\{{\sf P}\})}\sim\inf\left\{{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[T]:\;(T,D)\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)\right\}.

The proof can be found in Appendix
D.1
.
∎

We continue with the case that there is also a detection task, i.e.,

\emptyset\notin\Psi
, where we compare the three tests,
\chi^{*}_{det}
,
\chi^{*}
and
\chi^{*}_{fwer}
, introduced in Subsections
3.2.2
,
3.2.3
and
3.2.4
, respectively. We start by establishing sufficient conditions for their asymptotic optimality when there are no signals present.

Theorem 4.4
.

Suppose that
\emptyset\in\Psi
,
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
, and let
the detection subsystems be selected such that

\min_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\;\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e};s_{e}\right)=\min_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\;\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}\right),

(17)

which is trivially the case when
s_{e}=[K]
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
.
Then, as
\alpha,\beta\to 0
,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}_{det}\right]

\displaystyle\;\sim\;\max_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\left\{\frac{|\log\beta|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}\right)}\right\}\;\sim\;\inf\left\{{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[T]:\;(T,D)\in\mathcal{D}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta)\right\},

as
\alpha,\beta\to 0
, while
\gamma
and
\delta
are either fixed or go to
0
,

{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}\right]\;\sim\;\max_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\left\{\frac{|\log\beta|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}\right)}\right\}\;\sim\;\inf\left\{{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[T]:\;(T,D)\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)\right\},

and as
\gamma,\delta\to 0
,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}_{fwer}\right]\;\sim\;\max_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\left\{\frac{|\log\delta|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}\right)}\right\}\;\sim\;\inf\left\{{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[T]:\;(T,D)\in\mathcal{E}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)\right\}.

The proof can be found in Appendix
D.4
.
∎

Theorem
4.4
shows that, with sufficiently large subsystems so that (
17
) holds,
\chi^{*}
has
the same expected sample size as
\chi^{*}_{det}
, to a first-order approximation as
\alpha
and
\beta
go to
0
, independently of how
\gamma
and
\delta
are selected. On the other hand, this is the case for
\chi^{*}_{fwer}
when
\delta=\beta
, in which case its missed detection rate does not exceed
\beta
(recall (
3
)). Therefore, when
\delta=\beta
and
there are not any signals present
,

\chi^{*}_{fwer}
does not lead to deterioration in performance in comparison to
\chi^{*}_{det}
, when
\alpha
and
\beta
are small enough.

We continue with the case that there is at least one signal.

Theorem 4.5
.

Suppose that
\emptyset\in\Psi
,
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}
, and let the detection subsystems be selected so that

\max_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0};s_{e})=\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0}),

(18)

which is trivially the case when
s_{e}=[K]
for some
e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})
. Then, as
\alpha,\beta\to 0
,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}_{det}\right]

\displaystyle\sim\frac{|\log\alpha|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0}\right)}\sim\inf\left\{{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[T]:\;(T,D)\in\mathcal{D}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta)\right\},

and, as
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that

|\log\alpha|>>|\log\gamma|\vee|\log\delta|,

{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}\right]\sim\frac{|\log\alpha|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0}\right)}\sim\inf\left\{{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[T]:\;(T,D)\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)\right\}.

If, also, the isolation subsystems are selected so that (
15
) holds, then, as
\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that
|\log\gamma|>>|\log\delta|
,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}_{fwer}\right]\sim\max_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\gamma|}{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0})\wedge\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e})}\right\}\sim\inf\left\{{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[T]:\;(T,D)\in\mathcal{E}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)\right\}.

The proof can be found in Appendix
D.4
.
∎

Theorem
4.5
shows that, when the subsystems are large enough so that (
18
) holds,
\chi^{*}
has the same expected sample size as
\chi^{*}_{det}
to a first-order approximation as
\alpha
goes to
0
much faster than
\gamma
and
\delta
.
On the other hand, when
\gamma=\alpha
, in which case the false alarm rate of

\chi^{*}_{fwer}
does not exceed
\alpha
(recall (
3
)), then from the the above theorem it follows that

\frac{{\sf E}_{\sf P}[T^{*}_{det}]}{{\sf E}_{\sf P}[T_{fwer}^{*}]}\to\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{P})}\frac{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e})}{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0})}\wedge 1

as
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that

|\log\alpha|>>|\log\gamma|\vee|\log\delta|
. Intuitively speaking, this means that
\chi^{*}_{fwer}
has the same asymptotic performance as

\chi^{*}_{det}
when there is at least one signal and
\gamma=\alpha
is much smaller than
\delta
, as long as the detection problem is harder than the isolation problem in the sense that

\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0})\leq\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e}).

Typically, however, especially when there are more than one signals under
{\sf P}
, the isolation problem is (much) harder, and hence, the above limit will be (much) smaller than 1, suggesting that when
\gamma=\alpha
is much smaller than
\delta
,
\chi^{*}_{fwer}
will perform much worse than
\chi^{*}_{det}
.

Finally, we consider the general case where all error rates go to
0
at arbitrary rates, and there is at least one signal.

Theorem 4.6
.

Suppose that
\emptyset\in\Psi
,
{\sf P}\in{\sf P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}
, and let the detection subsystems be selected so that (
18
) holds,
and let the isolation subsystems be selected so that (
15
) - (
16
) hold. Then, as
\gamma,\delta\to 0
,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}_{fwer}\right]

\displaystyle\sim\max_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\gamma|}{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0})\wedge\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e})}\right\}\bigvee\max_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\delta|}{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e})}\right\}

\displaystyle\sim\inf\left\{{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[T]:\;(T,D)\in\mathcal{E}_{\Psi}(\gamma,\delta)\right\},

and, as
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
,

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}\right]

\displaystyle\sim\frac{|\log\alpha|}{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0})}\bigvee\max_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\gamma|}{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e})}\right\}\bigvee\max_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\delta|}{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e})}\right\}

\displaystyle\sim\inf\left\{{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[T]:\;(T,D)\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)\right\}.

The proof can be found in Appendix
D.5
.
∎

Theorem
4.6
generalizes
Theorem
4.5
and allows for
a comparison between
\chi^{*}
and
\chi^{*}_{fwer}
as
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta
go to 0 at arbitrary rates. For example, an implication of this theorem is that,
when the detection and isolation subsystems are large enough so that conditions (
18
) and (
15
)-(
16
) hold,
\chi^{*}
and
\chi^{*}_{fwer}
have the same expected sample size, to a first-order asymptotic approximation, when there is at least one signal and either
\alpha=\gamma,\delta\to 0
or
\delta
goes to
0
much faster than
\alpha
and
\gamma
.

Remark 4.3
.

In Theorems
4.3
and
4.6
, the isolation subsystems do not need to satisfy (
16
) (resp. (
15
)) when
|\log\gamma|>>|\log\delta|
(resp.
|\log\delta|>>|\log\gamma|
). The proof for this claim is presented in Appendix
D.6
.

Remark 4.4
.

For the asymptotic approximation regarding the expected sample size of
T^{*}_{fwer}
in Theorems
4.5
and
4.6
, the isolation subsystems do not need to satisfy (
15
) when

\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0})\leq\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e})\quad\text{and}\quad\max_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0};s_{e})\leq\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e};s^{\prime}_{e}\right),

(19)

and the detection subsystems do not need to satisfy (
18
) when

\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0})\geq\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e})\quad\text{and}\quad\max_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0};s_{e})\geq\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e};s^{\prime}_{e}\right).

(20)

The proof for this claim is presented in Appendix
D.7
.

Remark 4.5
.

The conditions of the previous theorems may be satisfied when the detection and isolation subsystems are strict subsets of
[K]
, even when
s_{e}=s^{\prime}_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
. We elaborate this point in Proposition
D.1
of the supplementary file, as well as in the two following sections, where we specialize the general setup which is considered so far.

5
Detection and isolation of anomalous sources

In this section we apply the results of Sections
2
-
4
to the problem of detecting and isolating anomalous sources. That is, throughout this section the units are the data sources themselves, i.e.,
\mathcal{K}=\{\{k\}:k\in[K]\}
and, as a result, the hypotheses refer to the marginal distributions of the data sources. Moreover, we assume that the distributional assumptions (
11
)-(
12
) are satisfied, thus, all asymptotic optimality results in Section
4
hold when the subsystems are large enough.

In Subsection
5.1
we consider the case that the data sources are independent, i.e.,

{\sf P}={\sf P}^{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes{\sf P}^{K}\quad\text{ for every }\;{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi},

(21)

and there are a priori bounds on the number of signals, i.e.,
\Psi
is of the form

\Psi_{l,u}
, and we show that the asymptotically optimal procedures in this context are also
easily implementable
. In Subsection
5.2
we consider the general case where the data sources are not necessarily independent,
and asymptotically optimal procedures are not, in general, easy to implement. In this context, we focus on computationally simple versions of the proposed tests, upper bound their asymptotic relative efficiencies, and establish sufficient conditions for their asymptotic optimality.

Remark 5.1
.

An implication of the independence assumption (
21
) is that conditions (
11
)-(
12
),
which refer to
global
distributions, are satisfied as long as similar conditions are satisfied by the marginal distributions of the data sources. For example, they hold when,
for every
k\in[K]
,
X^{k}
is an iid sequence and
Z_{1}({\sf P}^{k},{\sf Q}^{k})
has finite, non-zero expectation under both
{\sf P}^{k}
and
{\sf Q}^{k}
. For more details on this point, we refer to Proposition
E.1
.

5.1
The case of independence

Throughout this subsection we assume that the data sources are independent, i.e., (
21
) holds, and that arbitrary lower and upper bounds,
l
and
u
, are given on the number of signals, i.e.,
\Psi=\Psi_{l,u}
. In this setup, we show that asymptotically optimal procedures are always easy to implement, as they only require
ordering at each time
n\in\mathbb{N}
the local statistics
\Lambda_{k}(n),k\in[K]
, defined in (
5
).

We start by showing that, even when the isolation subsystems are selected as large as possible, i.e.,
s^{\prime}_{k}=[K]
for every
k\in[K]
, the estimated signal set, defined in (
4
), at
T^{*}
or
T^{*}_{fwer}
, admits a very simple form.

Theorem 5.1
.

Suppose that either
s^{\prime}_{k}=\{k\}\;\forall\;k\in[K]
, or
s^{\prime}_{k}=[K]\;\forall\;k\in[K]
. When
l=0
, suppose also that either
s_{k}=\{k\}\;\forall\;k\in[K]
, or
s_{k}=[K]\;\forall\;k\in[K]
. Then:

D_{\rm iso}(T)=\left\{i_{1}(T),\dots,i_{l\vee p(T)\wedge u}(T)\right\}\quad\text{for}\;\;T\in\{T^{*},T^{*}_{fwer}\},

with the understanding that if
l=p(T)=0
, then
D_{\rm iso}(T)=\emptyset
.

When
s^{\prime}_{k}=\{k\}
for every
k\in[K]
, the proof is straightforward. Indeed, in this case the isolation statistics reduce to the local statistics and
D_{\rm iso}(T)=\left\{i_{1}(T),\dots,i_{p(T)}(T)\right\}
. At the same time, at least
l
and at most
u
many local statistics are greater than
1
at the time of stopping, i.e.,
l\leq p(T)\leq u
. When
s^{\prime}_{k}=[K]
for every
k\in[K]
, on the other hand, the proof is much more involved and is presented in Appendix
E.3
.
∎

In the rest of this subsection we set either
s^{\prime}_{k}=\{k\}
for every
k\in[K]
or
s^{\prime}_{k}=[K]
for every
k\in[K]
, and focus on the form of the stopping rules. We start with the case that
l\geq 1
, where there is no detection task.

Theorem 5.2
.

Suppose that
l\geq 1
and let
s^{\prime}_{k}=[K]
for every
k\in[K]
.

(i)

If
\ell=u<K
, then

\displaystyle T^{*}

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\Lambda_{(u)}(n)\geq\Lambda_{(u+1)}(n)\;\max\{A,B\}\right\}.

(ii)

If
l<u\leq K
, then

\displaystyle T^{*}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}T_{1}\wedge T_{2}\wedge T_{4}\wedge T_{5}\wedge T_{6}&\text{if}\quad u<K\\
T_{1}\wedge T_{2}\wedge T_{3}&\text{if}\quad u=K\\
\end{cases},

where

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;p(n)<l\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda_{(l)}(n)\geq\Lambda_{(l+1)}(n)\;\max\{A,B\}\right\},

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;p(n)=l\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda_{(l+1)}(n)\leq\min\left\{1/A,\;\Lambda_{(l)}(n)/B\right\}\right\},

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;p(n)>l\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda_{k}(n)\notin\left(1/A,B\right)\quad\forall\;k\in[K]\right\},

\displaystyle T_{4}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;p(n)\in(l,u)\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda_{k}(n)\notin\left(1/A,B\right)\quad\forall\;k\in[K]\right\},

\displaystyle T_{5}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;p(n)=u\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda_{(u)}(n)\geq\max\left\{B,\;A\,\Lambda_{(u+1)}(n)\right\}\right\},

\displaystyle T_{6}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;p(n)>u\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda_{(u)}(n)\geq\Lambda_{(u+1)}(n)\;\max\{A,B\}\right\}.

The proof can be found in Appendix
E.4
.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

When
l=u
, the stopping time in Theorem
5.2
coincides with the so-called “gap rule”, which was proposed in
[
34
]
and where its asymptotic optimality was established only in the case that the sources generate iid data. When
l<u
, on the other hand, the stopping time in Theorem
5.2
differs from the “gap-intersection rule” proposed in
[
34
]
. In particular, it is defined up to only 2, instead of 4, distinct thresholds, while being the minimum of 6, instead of 3, stopping times.

We continue with the case that
l=0
, where there is also a detection task.

Theorem 5.3
.

Suppose that
l=0
and
s_{k}=[K]
for every
k\in[K]
. Then, the stopping times,
T_{0}
and
T_{det}
, defined as in Subsection
3.2.2
, take the following form:

\displaystyle T_{0}

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;\Lambda_{(1)}(n)\leq 1/C\right\},\quad T_{det}=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;\prod_{i=1}^{p(n)\wedge u}\Lambda_{(i)}(n)\geq D\}.

If, also,
s^{\prime}_{k}=[K]
for every
k\in[K]
, then

\displaystyle T_{joint}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}T_{1}\wedge T_{3}\wedge T_{4}\wedge T_{5}&\text{if}\quad u<K\\
T_{1}\wedge T_{2}&\text{if}\quad u=K\end{cases},

where

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;\Lambda_{(1)}(n)\geq D,\quad\Lambda_{(2)}(n)\leq\min\{1/A,\;\Lambda_{(1)}(n)/B\}\},

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;p(n)>1,\quad\prod_{i=1}^{p(n)}\Lambda_{(i)}(n)\geq D,\quad\Lambda_{k}(n)\notin\left(1/A,B\right)\quad\forall\;k\in[K]\},

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;p(n)\in(1,u),\quad\prod_{i=1}^{p(n)}\Lambda_{(i)}(n)\geq D,\quad\Lambda_{k}(n)\notin\left(1/A,B\right)\quad\forall\;k\in[K]\},

\displaystyle T_{4}

\displaystyle=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;p(n)=u,\quad\prod_{i=1}^{u}\Lambda_{(i)}(n)\geq D,\quad\Lambda_{(u)}(n)\geq\max\left\{B,\;A\,\Lambda_{(u+1)}(n)\right\}\},

\displaystyle T_{5}

\displaystyle=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;p(n)>u,\quad\prod_{i=1}^{u}\Lambda_{(i)}(n)\geq D,\quad\Lambda_{(u)}(n)\geq\Lambda_{(u+1)}(n)\max\{A,B\}\}.

The proof can be found in Appendix
E.5
.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

The corresponding rule for the pure detection problem has been considered in
[
39
,
38
,
41
,
17
]
.

Subsection
3.2.4
implies that setting
A=C
and
B=D
in Theorem
5.3
leads to an asymptotically optimal test with respect to
familywise error control
, since
s_{k}=s^{\prime}_{k}=[K]
for every
k\in[K]
. In the next theorem we show that such an asymptotically optimal, and
equally feasible
, test can also be obtained even with

s_{k}=\{k\}
and
s^{\prime}_{k}=\{k\}
(resp.
[K]
) for every
k\in[K]
when
u=K
(resp.
u<K
).

Theorem 5.4
.

Suppose that
l=0
and
s_{k}=\{k\}
for every
k\in[K]
. Then
\chi^{*}_{fwer}
is asymptotically optimal, as
\gamma,\delta\to 0
, under every
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}

(i)

when
u=K
and
s^{\prime}_{k}=\{k\}
for every
k\in[K]
, in which case

\displaystyle T^{*}_{fwer}

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;\Lambda_{k}(n)\notin\left(1/A,B\right)\quad\forall\;k\in[K]\right\},

(ii)

when
u<K
and
s^{\prime}_{k}=[K]
for every
k\in[K]
, in which case

\displaystyle T^{*}_{fwer}

\displaystyle=T_{0}\wedge T_{1}\wedge T_{2}\wedge T_{3}\wedge T_{4},

where

\displaystyle T_{0}

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;\Lambda_{(1)}(n)\leq 1/A\right\},

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;\Lambda_{(1)}(n)\geq B,\quad\Lambda_{(2)}(n)\leq\min\{1/A,\;\Lambda_{(1)}(n)/B\}\right\},

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;p(n)\in(1,u),\quad\Lambda_{(1)}(n)\geq B,\quad\Lambda_{k}(n)\notin\left(1/A,B\right)\quad\forall\;k\in[K]\right\},

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;p(n)=u,\quad\Lambda_{(u)}(n)\geq\max\left\{B,\;A\,\Lambda_{(u+1)}(n)\right\}\right\},

\displaystyle T_{4}

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;p(n)>u,\quad\Lambda_{(1)}(n)\geq B,\quad\Lambda_{(u)}(n)\geq\Lambda_{(u+1)}(n)\;\max\{A,B\}\right\}.

The proof can be found in Appendix
E.7
.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

When
u=K
, the stopping rule in Theorem
5.4
coincides with the “Intersection rule”, proposed in
[
15
,
14
]
, whose asymptotic optimality was established in
[
34
,
35
]
.
When
u<K
, on the other hand, the stopping rule in Theorem
5.4
differs from the “gap-intersection” rule, which was proposed and analyzed in the iid setup in
[
34
]
, in that it is defined up to 2, instead of 4, distinct thresholds, while being the minimum of 5, instead of 3, stopping times.

5.2
The general case

In this subsection we do not assume that the independence assumption (
21
) holds, and we do not make any assumption regarding the form of the prior information,
\Psi
. We only assume that
\emptyset\in\Psi
, so that the detection problem is relevant, and focus on the joint detection and isolation problem, restricting our attention to the test
\chi^{*}
, introduced in Subsection
3.2.3
.

As mentioned earlier, if the subsystems are large enough,
\chi^{*}
is asymptotically optimal, but may not always be easy to implement in this general setup. For this reason, we focus on the case that the subsystems are selected as small as possible, i.e.,
s_{k}=s^{\prime}_{k}=\{k\}
for every
k\in[K]
, so that the implementation of
\chi^{*}
requires only the ordering of the local statistics, introduced in (
5
). For this version of
\chi^{*}
, we upper bound the asymptotic relative efficiency,

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]:=\limsup\;\frac{{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[T^{*}]}{\inf\left\{{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[T]:\;(T,D)\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)\right\}},

(22)

as
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta
go to 0 at various relative rates, for different
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
and
\Psi
, and establish sufficient conditions for its asymptotic optimality. Moreover, we specialize the above results to the problem of detecting and identifying non-zero Gaussian means.

We start with the asymptotic regime where
\gamma
(resp.
\delta
) goes to 0 much faster than
\alpha
and
\delta
(resp.
\gamma
). If, in particular, it is assumed that there can be at most
u
signals, i.e.,
\Psi=\Psi_{0,u}
, we show that the computationally simplest version of
\chi^{*}
is asymptotically optimal when the true number of signals is larger than
1
(resp. smaller than
u
), as long as one of the most difficult to isolate sources
is a signal (resp. non-signal) independent of the other sources.

Theorem 5.5
.

Suppose that
\emptyset\in\Psi
,
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
s^{\prime}_{k}=\{k\}
for every
k\in[K]
.

(i)

If
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that
|\log\gamma|>>|\log\alpha|\vee|\log\delta|
, then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\frac{\min_{\{k\}\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,k}\right)}{\min_{\{k\}\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{k},\mathcal{H}^{k}\right)}.

If, also,
\Psi=\Psi_{0,u}
,
|\mathcal{A}({\sf P})|>1
, and there is a source that is independent of the other ones under
{\sf P}
and achieves

\min_{\{k\}\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\;\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{k},\mathcal{H}^{k})
, then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1.

(ii)

If
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that
|\log\delta|>>|\log\alpha|\vee|\log\gamma|
, then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\frac{\min_{\{k\}\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,k}\right)}{\min_{\{k\}\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{k},\mathcal{G}^{k}\right)}.

If also
\Psi=\Psi_{0,u}
,
|\mathcal{A}({\sf P})|<u
, and there is a source that is independent of the other ones under
{\sf P}
and achieves
\min_{\{k\}\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\;\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{k},\mathcal{G}^{k})
, then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1.

The proof can be found in Appendix
F.1
.
∎

We next consider the asymptotic regime where
\alpha
is much smaller than
\gamma
and
\delta
, and we show that
the computationally simplest version of
\chi^{*}

is asymptotically optimal when (i) there is exactly one signal that is independent of the other sources, as well as when (ii)
\Psi_{1,1}\subseteq\Psi
, there is no signal, and one of the most difficult to isolate sources
is independent of the rest.

Theorem 5.6
.

Suppose that
\emptyset\in\Psi
and
s_{k}=\{k\}
for every
k\in[K]
.

(i)

If
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that

|\log\alpha|>>|\log\gamma|\vee|\log\delta|,
then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\min_{\{k\}\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0})}{\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{k},\mathcal{H}^{k})}\right\}.

If, also, there is exactly one signal that is independent of the other sources under
{\sf P}
, then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1.

(ii)

If
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
\alpha,\beta\to 0
, while
\gamma
and
\delta
are either fixed or go to
0
, then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\frac{\min_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,k}\right)}{\min_{k\in[K]}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{k},\mathcal{G}^{k}\right)}.

If, also,
\Psi_{1,1}\subseteq\Psi
, and there is a source that is independent of the other ones under
{\sf P}
and achieves

\min_{k\in[K]}\;\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{k},\mathcal{G}^{k})
, then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1.

The proof can be found in Appendix
F.2
.
∎

5.3
Detection and isolation of non-zero Gaussian means

We next specialize Theorems
5.5
and
5.6
to the testing problem in Subsection
2.4.1
.

Corollary 5.1
.

Consider the setup of Subsection
2.4.1
, and suppose that
\Psi=\Psi_{0,u}
and

s_{k}=s^{\prime}_{k}=\{k\}
for every
k\in[K]
. Fix
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
and let
R
be the correlation matrix and
\sigma_{k}^{2}
(resp.
\mu_{k}
) be the variance (resp. mean) of
X_{1}^{k}
for every
k\in[K]
(resp.
\{k\}\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})
) under
{\sf P}
.

(i)

If
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that
|\log\gamma|>>|\log\alpha|\vee|\log\delta|
and
|\mathcal{A}({\sf P})|>1
, then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\min\left\{R^{-1}_{jj}:\{j\}\in\argmin_{\{k\}\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\frac{\mu_{k}^{2}}{\sigma_{k}^{2}}\right\}.

Moreover,
{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1
when there is a source that is independent of others and achieves

\min_{\{k\}\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}({\mu_{k}^{2}}/{\sigma_{k}^{2}}).

(ii)

If
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that
|\log\delta|>>|\log\alpha|\vee|\log\gamma|
and
1\leq|\mathcal{A}({\sf P})|<u
, then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\min\left\{R^{-1}_{jj}:\{j\}\in\argmin_{\{k\}\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\min_{\theta\in\mathcal{M}_{k}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\theta^{2}}{\sigma_{k}^{2}}\right\}.

Moreover,
{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1
when there is a source that is independent of others and achieves

\min_{\{k\}\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\min_{\theta\in\mathcal{M}_{k}\setminus\{0\}}({\theta^{2}}/{\sigma_{k}^{2}}).

(iii)

If
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that

|\log\alpha|>>|\log\gamma|\vee|\log\delta|,
then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\sum_{\{i\},\{j\}\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\frac{\mu_{i}R^{-1}_{ij}\mu_{j}}{\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}\bigg/\max_{\{k\}\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\frac{\mu_{k}^{2}}{\sigma_{k}^{2}}.

(iv)

If
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
\alpha,\beta\to 0
, while
\gamma
and
\delta
are either fixed or go to
0
, then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\min\left\{R^{-1}_{jj}:j\in\argmin_{k\in[K]}\min_{\theta\in\mathcal{M}_{k}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\theta^{2}}{\sigma_{k}^{2}}\right\}.

Moreover,
{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1
when there is a source that is independent of others and achieves

\min_{k\in[K]}\min_{\theta\in\mathcal{M}_{k}\setminus\{0\}}({\theta^{2}}/{\sigma_{k}^{2}}).

The proof of the upper bounds in (i)-(iv) can be found in Appendix
F.3
. To show when these upper bounds are equal to 1 and, as a result,
{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1
, we recall that no diagonal entry of the inverse of correlation matrix is smaller than
1
, i.e.,
R^{-1}_{jj}\geq 1
for every
j\in[K]
, and that the equality holds if and only if source
j
is independent of the other sources. In fact, this condition for asymptotic optimality
is more generally stated, for example, in Theorem
5.5
(i).
∎

6
Detection and isolation of a dependence structure

We next apply the general theory, developed in Sections
2
-
4
, to the problem of detecting and/or identifying dependent sources. To be specific, throughout this section the units are pairs of data sources, i.e.,

\mathcal{K}=\big\{\{i,j\}:1\leq i<j\leq K\big\},

and the components of
X^{e}
are independent (resp. dependent) under
{\sf P}
when
{\sf P}^{e}
belongs to
\mathcal{H}^{e}
(resp.
\mathcal{G}^{e}
)
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
and
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
. We also assume that conditions (
11
) and (
12
) are satisfied and, as a result, all asymptotic optimality results in Section
4
hold when the detection and isolation subsystems are equal to
[K]
. The next theorem provides a setup where this choice of subsystems leads to a test whose implementation has relatively low complexity.
For this result, as well as some others in this section, we also assume that, under any
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
,

\displaystyle\;\;s_{1},s_{2}\subseteq[K]\;\;\text{are disjoint and independent}\;\;

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\{i,j\}\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})\quad\forall\;i\in s_{1},j\in s_{2}.

(23)

This assumption is satisfied, for example, in the Gaussian setup of Subsection
2.4.2
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Suppose that (
23
) holds and
\Psi=\Psi_{dis}
. If
s_{e}=[K]
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
, then

\displaystyle T_{0}

\displaystyle=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\Lambda_{(1)}(n)\leq 1/C\},

\displaystyle T_{det}

\displaystyle=\inf\{n\in\mathbb{N}:\;\max_{\mathcal{B}\subseteq\{i_{1}(n),\dots i_{p(n)}(n)\},\;\mathcal{B}\in\Psi}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{B}}\Lambda_{e}(n)\geq D\},

where
T_{0}
and
T_{det}
are defined as in Subsection
3.2.2
.

The proof can be found in Appendix
G.1
.
∎

In general, however, setting
s_{e}=s^{\prime}_{e}=[K]
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
may not lead to computationally simple tests. For this reason, in the remainder of this section we obtain sufficient conditions for asymptotic optimality when the subsystems are smaller than
[K]
, and especially when
s_{e}=s^{\prime}_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
. When these conditions are not satisfied, we upper bound the asymptotic relative efficiency of
\chi^{*}
, in its simplest version.
Moreover, we focus on the joint detection and isolation problem, thus, we assume that
\emptyset\in\Psi
, and restrict our attention to the test
\chi^{*}
, introduced in Subsection
3.2.3
. We also note that, for every
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}
,
there is a disjoint partition of
[K]
,
v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{L({\sf P})}
, such that

{\sf P}={\sf P}^{v_{0}}\otimes{\sf P}^{v_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes{\sf P}^{v_{L({\sf P})}},

where
v_{0}
is the subset of
[K]
that consists of the independent sources under
{\sf P}
. This decomposition is used in the statement of the main results of this section.

Remark 6.1
.

Suppose that (
23
) holds. If
\Psi=\Psi_{clus}
, then
v_{1},\dots,v_{L({\sf P})}
are clusters themselves. If
\Psi=\Psi_{dis}
, then
L({\sf P})=|\mathcal{A}({\sf P})|
and
|v_{l}|=2
for every
l\in[L({\sf P})]
.

6.1
General results

We start with the asymptotic regime where
\gamma
(resp.
\delta
) goes to 0 much faster than
\alpha
and
\delta
(resp.
\gamma
). In this setup, we show that
\chi^{*}
is asymptotically optimal when the isolation subsystem that corresponds to each dependent (resp. independent) pair contains the subsets in the partition that include that pair. Moreover, we show that
\chi^{*}
is asymptotically optimal even with
s^{\prime}_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
, whenever one of the most difficult to isolate, dependent (resp. independent) pairs is independent of all other data sources.

Theorem 6.2
.

Suppose that
\emptyset\in\Psi
,
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}
, and
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that
|\log\gamma|>>|\log\alpha|\vee|\log\delta|
.

(i)

If, for every
e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})
, there is an
l\in[L({\sf P})]
so that
s^{\prime}_{e}\supseteq v_{l}\supseteq e
, then
{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1
.

(ii)

If
s^{\prime}_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
, then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\frac{\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e}\right)}{\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{H}^{e}\right)}.

Moreover,
{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1
when one of the following set of conditions is satisfied:

•

(
23
) holds,
\Psi=\Psi_{dis}
,
|\mathcal{A}({\sf P})|>1
,

•

\Psi
is either
\Psi_{dis}
, or
\Psi_{clus}
, or the powerset of
\mathcal{K}
,
L({\sf P})>1
, and there is a pair of data sources that is independent of all other data sources under
{\sf P}
and achieves

\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\;\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{H}^{e}).

The proof can be found in Appendix
G.3
.
∎

Theorem 6.3
.

Suppose that
\emptyset\in\Psi
,
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}
, and
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that
|\log\delta|>>|\log\alpha|\vee|\log\gamma|
.

(i)

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1
when, for every
e=\{i,j\}\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})
,

s^{\prime}_{e}\supseteq\begin{cases}v_{l}\quad&\text{if}\quad v_{l}\supseteq e\quad\text{for some}\quad l\in[L({\sf P})],\\
v_{l}\cup v_{l^{\prime}}\quad&\text{if}\quad i\in v_{l}\quad\text{and}\quad j\in v_{l^{\prime}}\quad\text{for some}\quad l\neq l^{\prime},\quad l,l^{\prime}\in[L({\sf P})],\\
e\cup v_{l}\quad&\text{if}\quad i\;\;\text{or}\;\;j\in v_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad e\setminus v_{0}\in v_{l}\quad\text{for some}\quad l\in[L({\sf P})],\\
e\quad&\text{if}\quad v_{0}\supseteq e.\end{cases}

(ii)

If
s^{\prime}_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
, then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\frac{\min_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}\right)}{\min_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{G}^{e}\right)}.

Moreover,
{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1
when
\Psi
is either
\Psi_{dis}
, or
\Psi_{clus}
, or the powerset of
\mathcal{K}
, and there is a pair of data sources that is independent of all other data sources under
{\sf P}
and achieves

\min_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\;\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{G}^{e}).

The proof can be found in Appendix
G.4
.
∎

We next consider the asymptotic regime where
\alpha
goes to 0 much faster than
\gamma
and
\delta
. In this setup, we show that
\chi^{*}
is asymptotically optimal
when the detection subsystem that corresponds to some dependent pair contains all dependent sources. Moreover, we show that
\chi^{*}
is asymptotically optimal even with
s_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
, whenever there is exactly one dependent pair that is independent of all other data sources.

Theorem 6.4
.

Suppose that
\emptyset\in\Psi
,
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}
, and
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that
|\log\alpha|>>|\log\gamma|\vee|\log\delta|
.

(i)

If there is an
e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})
such that
s_{e}\supseteq v_{1}\cup\ldots\cup v_{L({\sf P})}
, then
{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1
.

(ii)

If
s_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
, then

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0})}{\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{H}^{e})}\right\}.

Moreover,
{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1
when there is exactly one dependent pair that is independent of all other data sources under
{\sf P}
, for example, when
(
23
) holds and
|\mathcal{A}({\sf P})|=1
.

The proof can be found in Appendix
G.5
.
∎

We next consider the case that there are no signals and we show that
\chi^{*}
is asymptotically optimal, with
s_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
, if it is
a priori
possible that there exists exactly one dependent pair of data sources, i.e.,
\Psi_{1,1}\subseteq\Psi
, and one of the most difficult pairs to detect is independent of all other data sources.

Theorem 6.5
.

Suppose that
\emptyset\in\Psi
,
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
, and
\alpha,\beta\to 0
while
\gamma
and
\delta
are either fixed or go to
0
.
If
s_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
then
