(s^{\prime}_{e})^{c}=\cup_{k\neq l}v_{k}\cup\{v_{0}\setminus e\}
, which is independent of
v_{l}
and
e\setminus v_{0}
. Finally in the fourth case, we have
(s^{\prime}_{e})^{c}=\cup_{k}v_{k}\cup\{v_{0}\setminus e\}
, which is independent of
e
. Therefore, from the above it is clear that
(s^{\prime}_{e})^{c}
is independent of
s^{\prime}_{e}
.
Furthermore, letting

{\sf Q}_{1}=\argmin_{{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},{\sf Q};s^{\prime}_{e}\right)\quad\text{implies that}\quad{\sf Q}_{1}^{s^{\prime}_{e}}=\argmin_{{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}^{s^{\prime}_{e}}}\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{s^{\prime}_{e}},{\sf Q}).

Since
{\sf Q}_{1}^{s^{\prime}_{e}}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}^{s^{\prime}_{e}}
, denoting
s=s^{\prime}_{e}
, we have
e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}})\in\Psi
. Thus,

{\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0},

which implies that (
47
) holds and the result follows.

(ii)

If
s^{\prime}_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
then the upper bound on
{\sf{ARE}}
follows from part (i).
Now, it suffices to show that (
15
) holds, i.e.,

\min_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{G}^{e}\right)=\min_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}\right),

which again is true from Proposition
D.1
if (
47
) holds.
Fix the pair
\bar{e}\in\mathcal{K}
such that

\bar{e}\in\argmin_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{G}^{e}),

and for which
s
is independent of
s^{c}
under
{\sf P}
, where
s=s^{\prime}_{\bar{e}}=\bar{e}
. Furthermore, for any
{\sf Q}_{1}
such that

{\sf Q}_{1}\in\argmin_{{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,\bar{e}}}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},{\sf Q};\bar{e}\right)\quad\text{implies that}\quad{\sf Q}_{1}^{\bar{e}}\in\mathcal{G}^{\bar{e}}.

Since
{\sf Q}_{1}^{\bar{e}}\in\mathcal{G}^{\bar{e}}
, we have
\bar{e}\in\mathcal{A}\left({\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}}\right)\in\Psi
when
\Psi
is either
\Psi_{dis}
, or
\Psi_{clus}
, or the powerset of
\mathcal{K}
. Thus,

{\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0},

which implies that (
46
) holds and the result follows.

∎

G.5
Proof of Theorem
6.4

We have
\emptyset\in\Psi
,
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}
, and
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that
|\log\alpha|>>|\log\gamma|\vee|\log\delta|
.

(i)

From Lemma
D.2
(iii) we have

\displaystyle\inf\left\{{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[T]:\;(T,D)\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)\right\}\gtrsim\;\frac{|\log\alpha|}{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0})},

and on the other hand, from Theorem
4.2
(ii) we have

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}\right]\lesssim\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{|\log\alpha|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0};s_{e}\right)}\right\}.

Thus, from definition we have

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\frac{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0}\right)}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0};s_{e}\right)}.

Since by definition
{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\geq 1
, to prove that
{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1
, it suffices to show that
(
18
) holds, i.e.,

\max_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0};s_{e})=\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0}).

In view of Lemma
G.1
(iv), the above is true if (
49
) in Proposition
D.1
holds when for some
e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})

s_{e}=v_{1}\cup\ldots\cup v_{L({\sf P})}.

Then for that
e
, we have
s_{e}^{c}=v_{0}
which is independent of
s_{e}
.
Furthermore, letting

{\sf Q}_{1}=\argmin_{{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},{\sf Q};s_{e}\right)\quad\text{implies that}\quad{\sf Q}_{1}^{s_{e}}=\argmin_{{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^{s_{e}}}\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{s_{e}},{\sf Q}).

Therefore, denoting
s=s_{e}
, we have
\mathcal{A}({\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}})=\emptyset
. Thus,

{\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}}\in\mathcal{H}_{0},

which implies that (
49
) holds and the result follows.

(ii)

If
s_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
then the upper bound on
{\sf{ARE}}
follows from part (i).
Now, suppose the only dependent pair under
{\sf P}
is
\bar{e}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{A}({\sf P})=\{\bar{e}\}
and the rest of the sources are independent. Clearly this is the case when (
23
) holds and
|\mathcal{A}({\sf P})|=1
. Then

{\sf P}={\sf P}^{v_{0}}\otimes{\sf P}^{v_{1}},

where
v_{1}=\bar{e}
and
v_{0}=(\bar{e})^{c}
.
Therefore, when
s_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
, we have
s_{\bar{e}}=\bar{e}=v_{1}
. Thus, asymptotic optimality follows by using part (i).

∎

G.6
Proof of Theorem
6.5

We have
\emptyset\in\Psi
,
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
, and
\alpha,\beta\to 0
while
\gamma
and
\delta
are either fixed or go to
0
.
Now, from Lemma
D.2
(iii) we have

\displaystyle\inf\left\{{\sf E}_{{\sf P}}[T]:\;(T,D)\in\mathcal{C}_{\Psi}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta)\right\}\gtrsim\;\max_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\left\{\frac{|\log\beta|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}\right)}\right\},

and on the other hand, since
s_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
, from Theorem
4.2
(ii) we have

\displaystyle{\sf E}_{\sf P}\left[T^{*}\right]\lesssim\max_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\left\{\frac{|\log\beta|}{\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{G}^{e}\right)}\right\}.

Thus, from definition we have

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\frac{\min_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}\right)}{\min_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{G}^{e}\right)}.

Since by definition
{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\geq 1
, to prove that
{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]=1
, it suffices to show that
(
17
) holds, i.e.,

\min_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\;\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{G}^{e}\right)=\min_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\;\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}\right).

In view of Lemma
G.1
(iii), the above is true if (
48
) in Proposition
D.1
holds when
s_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
.
Fix the pair
\bar{e}\in\mathcal{K}
such that

\bar{e}\in\argmin_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\;\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{G}^{e}\right),

and for which
s
is independent of
s^{c}
under
{\sf P}
, where
s=s_{\bar{e}}=\bar{e}
. Clearly this is the case when (
23
) holds.
Furthermore, for any
{\sf Q}_{1}
such that

{\sf Q}_{1}\in\argmin_{{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,\bar{e}}}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},{\sf Q};\bar{e}\right)\quad\text{implies that}\quad{\sf Q}_{1}^{\bar{e}}\in\mathcal{G}^{\bar{e}}.

Since
{\sf Q}_{1}^{\bar{e}}\in\mathcal{G}^{\bar{e}}
and
|\mathcal{A}({\sf P})|=0
, we have
|\mathcal{A}\left({\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}}\right)|=1
. Thus, by using the fact that
\Psi_{1,1}\subseteq\Psi
, we have

{\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0},

which implies that (
48
) holds and the result follows.

∎

G.7
Proof of Theorem
6.6

The following lemma is important in establishing Theorem
6.6
.

Lemma G.2
.

Suppose (
23
) holds,
\Psi=\Psi_{dis}
and
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}
. Then

\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0})=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{H}^{e}).

Due to (
23
) there exists
{\sf P}_{0}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
, such that

{\sf P}=\bigotimes_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}{\sf P}^{e}\otimes{\sf P}_{0}^{v_{0}},\quad\text{where}\;\;v_{0}=[K]\setminus\bigcup_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}e.

Fix any
{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
. Since
\mathcal{A}({\sf Q})=\emptyset
, again due to (
23
) one can write
{\sf Q}
as follows:

{\sf Q}=\bigotimes_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}{\sf Q}^{e}\otimes{\sf Q}^{v_{0}}.

Then by Lemma
B.3
we have

\mathcal{I}({\sf P},{\sf Q})=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},{\sf Q}^{e}\right)+\mathcal{I}({\sf P},{\sf Q};v_{0}).

Therefore, the minimum of
\mathcal{I}({\sf P},{\sf Q})
over
{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
is obtained by
{\sf Q}_{*}
such that for every
e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})

{\sf Q}_{*}^{e}\in\argmin_{{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{H}^{e}}\;\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},{\sf Q}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad{\sf Q}_{*}^{v_{0}}={\sf P}^{v_{0}},

which yields

\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0})=\mathcal{I}({\sf P},{\sf Q}_{*})=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},{\sf Q}_{*}^{e}\right)+\mathcal{I}({\sf P},{\sf Q}_{*};v_{0})=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{H}^{e}\right).

∎Now we are ready to prove Theorem
6.6
.

First, fix any arbitrary
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
. Then in order to prove that this rule is asymptotically optimal under
{\sf P}
as
\gamma,\delta\to 0
, it suffices to show that (
48
) holds when
s_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
since by Proposition
D.1
, this will further imply that (
17
) in Theorem
4.4
is satisfied and the result will follow.
Fix
\bar{e}\in\mathcal{K}
such that

\bar{e}\in\argmin_{e\in\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{G}^{e}\right).

Since
\mathcal{A}({\sf P})=\emptyset
and (
23
) holds,
s
is independent of
s^{c}
, where
s=\bar{e}
. Furthermore, letting

{\sf Q}_{1}\in\argmin_{{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,\bar{e}}}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},{\sf Q};\bar{e}\right)\quad\text{implies that}\quad{\sf Q}_{1}^{\bar{e}}\in\mathcal{G}^{\bar{e}}.

This means under
{\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}}
there is exactly one dependent pair, i.e.,
\mathcal{A}\left({\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}}\right)=\{\bar{e}\}\in\Psi_{dis}
, which further implies

{\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}.

Thus, (
48
) holds.

Next, fix any arbitrary
{\sf P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}
.
We show that (
47
) holds when
s^{\prime}_{e}=e
for every
e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})
since by Proposition
D.1
, this will further imply that (
16
) is satisfied.
Fix the pair
\bar{e}\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})
such that

\bar{e}\in\argmin_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\;\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{G}^{e}\right),

and for which
s
is independent of
s^{c}
, where
s=s^{\prime}_{\bar{e}}=\bar{e}
.
Furthermore, letting

{\sf Q}_{1}=\argmin_{{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,\bar{e}}}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},{\sf Q};\bar{e}\right)\quad\text{implies that}\quad{\sf Q}_{1}^{\bar{e}}\in\mathcal{G}^{\bar{e}}.

Since under
{\sf P}^{s^{c}}
the dependent pairs among the sources
s^{c}
are still disjoint and
{\sf Q}_{1}^{\bar{e}}\in\mathcal{G}^{\bar{e}}
, we have
\bar{e}\in\mathcal{A}\left({\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}}\right)\in\Psi_{dis}
. Thus,

{\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0},

which implies (
47
) holds. Now we show that the conditions in Remark
4.4
are satisfied.

First, we consider the case when
|\mathcal{A}({\sf P})|=1
. Let
\mathcal{A}({\sf P})=\{e_{1}\}
.
Since every
{\sf Q}\in\cup_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e}
satisfies
\mathcal{A}({\sf P})\setminus\mathcal{A}({\sf Q})\neq\emptyset
, we have
e_{1}\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf Q})
, or
{\sf Q}^{e_{1}}\in\mathcal{H}^{e_{1}}
. Thus, by Lemma
B.2

\mathcal{I}({\sf P},{\sf Q})\geq\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e_{1}},{\sf Q}^{e_{1}})\geq\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e_{1}},\mathcal{H}^{e_{1}}),

and further taking infimum over
{\sf Q}\in\cup_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e}
in the left hand side yields

\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e})\geq\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e_{1}},\mathcal{H}^{e_{1}})=\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0}),

where the equality follows from Lemma
G.2
. The equality also implies that (
18
) in Theorem
4.5
is satisfied.
Furthermore, since
s_{e}=s^{\prime}_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
, we have

\max_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0};s_{e})=\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e_{1}},\mathcal{H}^{e_{1}})=\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e};s^{\prime}_{e}).

Thus, (
19
) holds.

Next, consider the case when
|\mathcal{A}({\sf P})|>1
. We will show that (
46
) holds when
s^{\prime}_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})
.
Fix
\bar{e}\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})
such that

\bar{e}\in\argmin_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\;\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{H}^{e}\right).

Now since the dependent pairs are disjoint and (
23
) holds, it is clear that
s
is independent of
s^{c}
, where
s=\bar{e}
. Furthermore, letting

{\sf Q}_{1}=\argmin_{{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,\bar{e}}}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},{\sf Q};\bar{e}\right)\quad\text{implies that}\quad{\sf Q}_{1}^{\bar{e}}\in\mathcal{H}^{\bar{e}}.

Since under
{\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}}
, the dependent pairs are still disjoint and
|\mathcal{A}\left({\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}}\right)|=|\mathcal{A}({\sf P})|-1\geq 1
, we have

{\sf Q}_{1}^{s}\otimes{\sf P}^{s^{c}}\in\mathcal{P}_{\Psi}\setminus\mathcal{H}_{0}.

Thus, (
46
) holds and by Proposition
D.1
this will further imply that (
15
) is satisfied. This means

\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e})=\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{H}^{e})\leq\sum_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{H}^{e})=\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0}),

where the last equality follows from Lemma
G.2
.
Furthermore, since
s_{e}=s^{\prime}_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
, we have

\max_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0};s_{e})=\max_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{H}^{e})\geq\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{H}^{e})=\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e};s^{\prime}_{e}).

Thus, (
20
) holds. Therefore, the asymptotic optimality of
\chi^{*}_{fwer}
follows by using Theorem
4.6
. The proof is complete.
∎

G.8
Proof of Corollary
6.1

We have
\Psi=\Psi_{clus}
and
s_{e}=s^{\prime}_{e}=e
for every
e\in\mathcal{K}
. Let
\mu
be the mean vector and
\Sigma
be the covariance matrix of
X_{n}
under
{\sf P}
. Then we have the decomposition that
\Sigma=URU
, where
U
is the diagonal matrix with elements of the main diagonal being the standard deviations of the sources, i.e.,

U={\rm{diag}}(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{K}),

and
R
is the correlation matrix.
For any
k\in[K]
we define

A_{k}=(1-\rho)I_{k}+\rho 1_{k}1_{k}^{t},

where

I_{k}
is the
k\times k
identity matrix, and
1_{k}
is the column vector in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
with all elements being
1
. Furthermore, for any
j,l\in[K]
we denote by
0_{j\times l}
the
j\times l
matrix with all entries being
0
. Without loss of generality let
v_{1}=\{1,2,\dots,m\}
, then

R=\begin{bmatrix}A_{m}&0_{m\times(K-m)}\\
0_{(K-m)\times m}&I_{K-m}\end{bmatrix}.

In the following we also use two important results regarding
A_{k}
:

\displaystyle A_{k}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\rho}I_{k}-\frac{\rho}{(1-\rho)(1+(k-1)\rho)}1_{k}1_{k}^{t},

\displaystyle det(A_{k})

\displaystyle=(1-\rho)^{k-1}(1+(k-1)\rho).

•

Consider the distribution
{\sf Q}
such that
L({\sf Q})=1
and the data sources
\{1,\dots,m-1\}
marginally follow equicorrelated multivariate Gaussian with correlation
\rho
and is independent of the rest of the sources. However, the mean vector of
X_{n}
and the variances of the data sources are the same under both
{\sf P}
and
{\sf Q}
. Then, denoting by
\Sigma_{{\sf Q}}
the covariance matrix of
X_{n}
under
{\sf Q}
, we have
\Sigma_{\sf Q}=UR_{\sf Q}U
, where
R_{\sf Q}
is the correlation matrix under
{\sf Q}
that takes the following form

R_{\sf Q}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{m-1}&0_{(m-1)\times(K-m+1)}\\
0_{(K-m+1)\times(m-1)}&I_{K-m+1}\end{bmatrix}.

Then we have

R_{\sf Q}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}B_{\sf Q}&0_{m\times(K-m)}\\
0_{(K-m)\times m}&I_{K-m}\end{bmatrix},\quad\text{where}\quad B_{\sf Q}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{m-1}^{-1}&0_{(m-1)\times 1}\\
0_{1\times(m-1)}&1\end{bmatrix},

which implies

R_{\sf Q}^{-1}R=\begin{bmatrix}B_{\sf Q}A_{m}&0_{m\times(K-m)}\\
0_{(K-m)\times m}&I_{K-m}\end{bmatrix}.

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle B_{\sf Q}A_{m}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{m-1}^{-1}&0_{(m-1)\times 1}\\
0_{1\times(m-1)}&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}A_{m-1}^{-1}&\rho 1_{m-1}\\
\rho 1_{m-1}^{t}&1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{m-1}&\rho A_{m-1}^{-1}1_{m-1}\\
\rho 1_{m-1}^{t}&1\end{bmatrix},

which yields
tr(B_{\sf Q}A_{m})=m
.
Now, from (
14
) we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}({\sf P},{\sf Q})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{det(\Sigma_{\sf Q})}{det(\Sigma)}-K+tr(\Sigma_{\sf Q}^{-1}\Sigma)+(\mu-\mu)^{t}\Sigma_{\sf Q}^{-1}(\mu-\mu)\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{det(UR_{\sf Q}U)}{det(URU)}-K+tr(U^{-1}R_{\sf Q}^{-1}U^{-1}URU)\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{(det(U))^{2}det(R_{\sf Q})}{(det(U))^{2}det(R)}-K+tr(UU^{-1}R_{\sf Q}^{-1}R)\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{det(R_{\sf Q})}{det(R)}-K+tr(R_{\sf Q}^{-1}R)\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{det(A_{m-1})}{det(A_{m})}-K+tr(B_{\sf Q}A_{m})+tr(I_{K-m})\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{-\log\frac{det(A_{m})}{det(A_{m-1})}-K+m+(K-m)\right\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{(1-\rho)^{m-1}(1+(m-1)\rho)}{(1-\rho)^{m-2}(1+(m-2)\rho)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{(1-\rho)(1+(m-1)\rho)}{(1+(m-2)\rho)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(1-\frac{\rho^{2}(m-1)}{1+(m-2)\rho}\right)

Now note that, the pair
\{m-1,m\}
is independent under
{\sf Q}
but dependent under
{\sf P}
, which implies
{\sf Q}\in\cup_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e}
.
Therefore, as
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that
|\log\gamma|>>|\log\alpha|\vee|\log\delta|
, from Theorem
6.2
(ii) we have

\displaystyle{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{\Psi,e}\right)}{\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{H}^{e}\right)}\leq\frac{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},{\sf Q})}{(-1/2)\log(1-\rho^{2})}

\displaystyle=\log\left(1-\rho\frac{\rho(m-1)}{1+\rho(m-2)}\right)/\log(1-\rho^{2}).

Since the above upper bound is an increasing function of
m
, one can let
m\to\infty
to obtain the following upper bound which is independent of
m
:

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\frac{\log(1-\rho)}{\log(1-\rho^{2})}.

•

Consider the distribution
{\sf Q}
such that
L({\sf Q})=1
and the data sources
\{1,\dots,m+1\}
marginally follow equicorrelated multivariate Gaussian with correlation
\rho
and is independent of the rest of the sources. However, the mean vector of
X_{n}
and the variances of the data sources are the same under both
{\sf P}
and
{\sf Q}
. Then, denoting by
\Sigma_{{\sf Q}}
the covariance matrix of
X_{n}
under
{\sf Q}
, we have
\Sigma_{\sf Q}=UR_{\sf Q}U
, where
R_{\sf Q}
is the correlation matrix under
{\sf Q}
that takes the following form

R_{\sf Q}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{m+1}&0_{(m+1)\times(K-m-1)}\\
0_{(K-m-1)\times(m+1)}&I_{K-m-1}\end{bmatrix}.

Then we have

R_{\sf Q}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{m+1}^{-1}&0_{(m+1)\times(K-m-1)}\\
0_{(K-m-1)\times(m+1)}&I_{K-m-1}\end{bmatrix},

which implies

R_{\sf Q}^{-1}R=\begin{bmatrix}A_{m+1}^{-1}B_{\sf P}&0_{(m+1)\times(K-m-1)}\\
0_{(K-m-1)\times(m+1)}&I_{K-m-1}\end{bmatrix},\quad\text{where}\quad B_{\sf P}=\begin{bmatrix}A_{m}&0_{m\times 1}\\
0_{1\times m}&1\end{bmatrix}.

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle A_{m+1}^{-1}B_{\sf P}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{1-\rho}I_{m+1}-\frac{\rho}{(1-\rho)(1+m\rho)}1_{m+1}1_{m+1}^{t}\right)B_{\sf P}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\rho}B_{\sf P}-\frac{\rho}{(1-\rho)(1+m\rho)}1_{m+1}1_{m+1}^{t}B_{\sf P},

which yields

\displaystyle tr(A_{m+1}^{-1}B_{\sf P})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\rho}tr(B_{\sf P})-\frac{\rho}{(1-\rho)(1+m\rho)}tr(1_{m+1}1_{m+1}^{t}B_{\sf P})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\rho}(tr(A_{m})+1)-\frac{\rho}{(1-\rho)(1+m\rho)}tr(1_{m+1}^{t}B_{\sf P}1_{m+1})

\displaystyle=\frac{m+1}{1-\rho}-\frac{\rho}{(1-\rho)(1+m\rho)}1_{m+1}^{t}B_{\sf P}1_{m+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{m+1}{1-\rho}-\rho\frac{m(1+(m-1)\rho)+1}{(1-\rho)(1+m\rho)}

Now, from (
14
) we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}({\sf P},{\sf Q})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{det(\Sigma_{\sf Q})}{det(\Sigma)}-K+tr(\Sigma_{\sf Q}^{-1}\Sigma)+(\mu-\mu)^{t}\Sigma_{\sf Q}^{-1}(\mu-\mu)\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{det(UR_{\sf Q}U)}{det(URU)}-K+tr(U^{-1}R_{\sf Q}^{-1}U^{-1}URU)\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{(det(U))^{2}det(R_{\sf Q})}{(det(U))^{2}det(R)}-K+tr(UU^{-1}R_{\sf Q}^{-1}R)\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{det(R_{\sf Q})}{det(R)}-K+tr(R_{\sf Q}^{-1}R)\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{det(A_{m+1})}{det(A_{m})}-K+tr(A_{m+1}^{-1}B_{\sf P})+tr(I_{K-m-1})\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigg\{\log\frac{(1-\rho)^{m}(1+m\rho)}{(1-\rho)^{m-1}(1+(m-1)\rho)}-K+K-m-1

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{m+1}{1-\rho}-\rho\frac{m(1+(m-1)\rho)+1}{(1-\rho)(1+m\rho)}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigg\{\log\frac{(1-\rho)(1+m\rho)}{(1+(m-1)\rho)}

\displaystyle+\frac{(m+1)(1+m\rho)-\rho(1+m+m(m-1)\rho)-(m+1)(1-\rho)(1+m\rho)}{(1-\rho)(1+m\rho)}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bigg\{-\log\frac{(1+(m-1)\rho)}{(1-\rho)(1+m\rho)}+\rho\frac{(m+1)(1+m\rho)-(1+m+m(m-1)\rho)}{(1-\rho)(1+m\rho)}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\bigg\{-\log\left(\frac{1+\rho(m-1)}{1+\rho(m-1)-\rho^{2}m}\right)+\frac{2m\rho^{2}}{(1-\rho)(1+m\rho)}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{1+\rho(m-1)}{1+\rho(m-1)-\rho^{2}m}\right)+\frac{m\rho^{2}}{(1+m\rho)(1-\rho)}

Now note that, the pair
\{m,m+1\}
is dependent under
{\sf Q}
but independent under
{\sf P}
, which implies
{\sf Q}\in\cup_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}
.
Therefore, as
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that
|\log\delta|>>|\log\alpha|\vee|\log\gamma|
, from Theorem
6.3
(ii) we have

\displaystyle{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\frac{\min_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P},\mathcal{G}_{\Psi,e}\right)}{\min_{e\notin\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\mathcal{I}\left({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{G}^{e}\right)}\leq\frac{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},{\sf Q})}{\log\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}+{\rho^{2}}/{(1-\rho^{2})}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{1+\rho(m-1)}{1+\rho(m-1)-\rho^{2}m}\right)+\frac{m\rho^{2}}{(1+m\rho)(1-\rho)}\right\}\bigg/\left\{\log\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{1-\rho^{2}}\right\}.

Since the above upper bound is an increasing function of
m
, one can let
m\to\infty
to obtain the following upper bound which is independent of
m
:

{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]\leq\left(\log(1-\rho)+\frac{2\rho}{1-\rho}\right)\bigg/\left(\log(1-\rho^{2})+\frac{2\rho^{2}}{1-\rho^{2}}\right).

•

Consider the distribution
{\sf Q}
such that all sources are independent. However, the mean vector of
X_{n}
and the variances of the data sources are the same under both
{\sf P}
and
{\sf Q}
. Then, denoting by
\Sigma_{{\sf Q}}
the covariance matrix of
X_{n}
under
{\sf Q}
, we have
\Sigma_{\sf Q}=U^{2}
.
Now, from (
14
) we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}({\sf P},{\sf Q})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{det(\Sigma_{\sf Q})}{det(\Sigma)}-K+tr(\Sigma_{\sf Q}^{-1}\Sigma)+(\mu-\mu)^{t}\Sigma_{\sf Q}^{-1}(\mu-\mu)\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{det(U^{2})}{det(URU)}-K+tr(U^{-1}U^{-1}URU)\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{(det(U))^{2}}{(det(U))^{2}det(R)}-K+tr(UU^{-1}R)\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\log\frac{1}{det(R)}-K+tr(R)\right\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\log(det(R))=-\frac{1}{2}\log(det(A_{m}))

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\log((1-\rho)^{m-1}(1+(m-1)\rho))

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left((m-1)\log(1-\rho)+\log(1+(m-1)\rho)\right)

Now, clearly
{\sf Q}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
.
Therefore, as
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\to 0
such that
|\log\alpha|>>|\log\gamma|\vee|\log\delta|
, from Theorem
6.4
(ii) we have

\displaystyle{\sf{ARE}}_{{\sf P}}[\chi^{*}]

\displaystyle\leq\min_{e\in\mathcal{A}({\sf P})}\left\{\frac{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},\mathcal{H}_{0})}{\mathcal{I}({\sf P}^{e},\mathcal{H}^{e})}\right\}\leq\frac{\mathcal{I}({\sf P},{\sf Q})}{(-1/2)\log(1-\rho^{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{(m-1)\log(1-\rho)+\log(1+(m-1)\rho)}{\log(1-\rho^{2})}.

The proof is complete.
∎
